# Knowledge Base: The Six Pillars of the Beast System

Project: the-beast-system. Built 2026-10-22. Seven papers: Part I financial-power, II political-power, III judicial-power, IV ideological-power, V coercive-power, VI productive-power, VII formalized-state ("The Formalized State: The Hierarchy of the Beast System").

**Thesis (revised per team/notation.md CENTRAL THESIS):** the ideology changes, the institutional functions remain. The Beast System is the universal six-function structure of organized governance (Proposition A, proved in Part VII from axioms); institutional capture (Proposition B) is contingent, measurable, and not implied by A.

## 0. How to use this file

1. Read Section 1 (guardrails) and Section 2 (source summary) first. Every Part uses them.
2. Go to your Part's section. Each has: guiding question, Necessity derivation, key concepts, six-regime table, candidate hypotheses, gaps, annotated bibliography.
3. Cite shared objects by the owner Part (Section 10).
4. Bibliography status tags are explained in Section 0.1; counts are in Section 11; BibTeX for everything is in Appendix B. **Only entries tagged VERIFIED-* were actually resolved by me on 2026-10-22.** Everything else carries an explicit warning tag. Do not upgrade a tag.

### 0.1 Verification protocol and status tags

| Tag | Meaning (what I actually did) |
|---|---|
| VERIFIED-DOI | DOI resolved at Crossref (api.crossref.org) and the returned title matched the entry title |
| VERIFIED-URL | URL returned HTTP 2xx or 3xx-followed-to-2xx when fetched |
| VERIFIED-CATALOG | No DOI. Title and first author found in the Open Library catalog (stable work URL given). This confirms the book exists with that title and author, NOT the page numbers, edition details or publisher string |
| DOI-CORRECTED | Supplied DOI failed; Crossref title search returned a different DOI whose title matched; the corrected DOI is used |
| DOI-MISMATCH / DOI-404 / URL-HTTP4xx | I supplied an identifier that did not resolve to the claimed work. Do NOT cite the identifier; use the catalog URL if present, otherwise treat as unverified |
| UNVERIFIED | Could not be confirmed by either route. Workers must check by web search before citing |
| NO-ID (book) | Pre-DOI or primary-text work; no DOI exists or none was found; bibliographic data are from memory of the standard edition |

Workers: the BibTeX blocks below are "BibTeX-ready" (key, type, author, year, title, venue fields). Page ranges and editions come from my memory unless the status says VERIFIED-DOI (Crossref metadata matched on title only; pages were not compared). Recheck pages before final submission.

## 1. CONTENT GUARDRAILS (every worker must obey)

1. **Revised guardrail 1 (notation.md wording):** "Beast System" names the universal six-function structure (Proposition A), present in every governance system. It is never a label for a particular religion, people, profession, regime or named person. Capture (Proposition B) is the contingent, evidence-based claim.
2. **Equal analytical treatment of six regime types.** Market-capitalist, communist party-state, democratic/market socialism and social democracy, Islamic states, historical Catholic monarchies, secular democracies get the same depth, the same questions, the same standard of evidence. Islam and Catholicism are treated as sources of political legitimacy in specific historical states, not as essences. Record internal diversity (Sunni/Shia, Ottoman/Safavid/Saudi/Iranian; Gallican/ultramontane, Habsburg/Bourbon/Papal; schools of jurisprudence; reform currents). No polemic, no ranking of civilizations.
3. **Measurement before accusation.** Differentiation does not equal capture (source Prop 1). Public goods are a rival explanation (source Prop 4, H6). Every claim needs a stated falsifier. Use the source's three labels: established institutional relationship / plausible but unconfirmed mechanism / normative or theological interpretation.
4. **No futurist identifications from Revelation.** Typological reading only. No present-day government, party, product, technology, payment system, vaccine or person is identified with the beast, mark or Babylon. Note preterist, typological/idealist and futurist readings and state which is used (typological).
5. **Slavery language is disciplined** (source Section 3): comparison with bondage is metaphorical; distinguish wage labor, taxation, serfdom, forced labor, chattel slavery.
6. **Citations identify arguments, not endorsement.** Contested works (Kuran, Kuru, Herman-Chomsky, Voslensky, Alfoneh, Gilens-Page) are paired with critics.
7. **Do not invent evidence or numbers.** U_t, I_t and weights are illustrative and must be preregistered (source Section 5.2). Never report a numeric U_t or I_t for a real country without a documented coding.

## 2. SOURCE SUMMARY: Largo (2026) [largo2026hbs]

### 2.1 Metadata
- **Title:** The Hierarchy of the Beast System: Institutional Power, Elite Coordination, and Economic Dependency Across Political Regimes. Subtitle: A Comparative Political-Economy Framework with a Theological Interpretation.
- **Author:** Mateo Largo (Independent Researcher). **Date:** 14 October 2026. 28 pages. Status: conceptual working paper, not peer reviewed, no original empirical estimates.
- **Keywords:** political economy; elite theory; state capture; institutional accountability; state socialism; Revelation.
- **Bib key (mandatory, from notation.md):** `largo2026hbs`.

### 2.2 Abstract (condensed)
HBS is a proposed analytical vocabulary for recurrent configurations of concentrated institutional power. The motivating image is a five-position order: resource-controlling elites; political/legal administrators; knowledge-producing and health-related institutions; coercive organizations; the working population. It can be investigated in capitalist, state-socialist and hybrid economies without presupposing a secret director, equivalence among institutions, or a verdict on professions. "Beast System" is a theological metaphor inspired by Revelation 13 and 17-18. A multiplex network model separates resource control, rule-making, legitimation, coercion and material reproduction, and distinguishes hierarchy, interdependence and capture. Conditional propositions on power concentration, institutional closure, information independence, coercive capacity, exit and voice. Conclusion: analogous mechanisms can arise under different ideologies, but the distribution of power, safeguards and public benefits differ substantially.

### 2.3 Section map of the source
1 Introduction (power vs domination vs coordination; four contributions) | 2 Literature (2.1 elite theory: Michels, Dahl, Bachrach-Baratz, Lukes; 2.2 institutional economics: Stigler, Olson, Acemoglu-Robinson, Hellman et al, North-Wallis-Weingast; 2.3 Weber, Mann, Svolik, Gerschewski, Foucault, Scott; 2.4 Hirschman, Ostrom, Habermas) | 3 Theological vocabulary | 4 Five-function model (R, L, K, E, W) | 5 Formal model | 6 Same functions under different orders | 7 Five mechanisms A-E | 8 Comparative illustrations (US, Soviet, post-socialist) | 9 Operationalization (constructs table, latent variable model, H1-H6, identification, falsification) | 10 Objections | 11 Accountability and redesign | 12 Discussion | 13 Conclusion | 14 Limitations | Appendices A data dictionary, B preregistration protocol, C decision rules, D primary-source inventory | References.

### 2.4 The five functions (source Section 4)
| Source symbol | Function | Content | Source caution |
|---|---|---|---|
| R | Command over scarce resources | capital, land, credit, investment, infrastructure, public budgets, strategic means of production | ownership, scale, or public-goods administration alone are not evidence of domination; in party-state R can sit under political authority |
| L | Rule production and administration | legislation, regulation, adjudication, taxation, licensing, administration | key indicator is selective distribution of discretion, burden and redress, not coerciveness of rules |
| K | Epistemic and cultural mediation | education, science, accreditation, media, professional expertise; healthcare and food only in their knowledge/standard-setting role | hospital is not a propaganda organ; professor is not an agent of command |
| E | Coercion and enforcement | police, military, prisons, intelligence | measured by discretionary and asymmetric deployment, not spending |
| W | Work, compliance, social reproduction | households, workers, taxpayers, small businesses, consumers, caregivers | active, not passive; taxation is not automatically extraction |

Series mapping (notation.md): R=F (Part I), L splits into P (Part II) and J (Part III), K=I (Part IV), E=C (Part V), W=W (Part VI). Source Figure 1 is a five-rung chain R > L > K > E > W with a dashed feedback arrow from W; the source calls it "a first approximation".

### 2.5 Formal objects (verbatim structure, series notation in brackets)
- **Polity network.** G_t = (V_t, E_t) directed multiplex; V_t organizations or coherent actor groups; five link types A^r (resource transfers and dependencies), A^l (rule-making authority), A^k (epistemic influence and communication), A^e (coercion and sanctions), A^w (labor, finance, material reproduction). [Series: six layers A^m_t, m in M={F,P,J,I,C,W}.]
- **Edge weight.** a^m_{ij,t} in [0,1]: documented capacity of actor i to constrain a feasible action of actor j through mechanism m. Conceptual unless measurement scheme fixed in advance.
- **Role map.** F: V -> 2^{R,L,K,E,W}, many-to-many. [Series: write the map as a role map and the financial pillar as \mathsf{F}.]
- **Capture link** (i -> j, m): (i) privileged access or discretion; (ii) the privilege weakens otherwise applicable public accountability or contestability; (iii) material evidence links the asymmetry to an institutional output. A disclosure-compliant donor meeting is not automatically capture.
- **Concentration.** H_{m,t} = sum_i (s^m_{i,t})^2 with sum_i s^m_{i,t} = 1 (HHI of decision-relevant control share).
- **Contestability.** Q_{m,t} in [0,1]: transparency, appeal rights, institutional independence, effective competition, credible replacement of decision makers.
- **Unaccountable concentration.** U_t = sum_{m} omega_m H_{m,t} (1 - Q_{m,t}), sum omega_m = 1, omega_m >= 0. "Illustrative index, not a validated measurement instrument"; weights preregistered; sensitivity analysis required.
- **Cross-domain integration.** C_{mn,t} in [0,1]: extent the same coalition commands appointments, budgets, sanctions or agenda in both m and n. **Integration statistic** I_t = (2 / (M(M-1))) sum_{m<n} C_{mn,t}. **The source has M = 5 (10 pairs); the series has M = 6 (15 pairs).** Write the statistic as \mathcal{I}_t to avoid clash with the ideological pillar label \mathsf{I}.
- **Strong HBS claim** requires both high U_t and high I_t. Section 9.5 adds a stricter classification: documented unaccountable concentration in at least two distinct functions; demonstrated cross-domain influence; persistent restriction of effective contestation; materially consequential policy or sanctioning pattern linked to the concentrated coalition.
- **Dynamics.** x_t = (R_t, L_t, K_t, E_t, W_t) aggregate capacities; x_{t+1} = B_t x_t + D_t z_t + eps_t; B_t inter-institutional dependencies, z_t external shocks, eps_t unobserved. Stability not inferable from positive links alone (negative feedback, substitution, saturation, legitimacy loss). Dominant eigenvalues of local linearization as language for persistence; B_t hard to estimate. [Series state vector: x_t = (F_t, P_t, J_t, I_t^cap, C_t, W_t).]
- **Coalition objective.** max_{p,c,g} Pi(p,c,g) = B(p) + rho(c) - kappa(g) - phi(opposition, oversight). p policy vector; c degree of coalition control; g coercive measures; B material/organizational benefit; rho rents from control; kappa direct and reputational cost of coercion; phi accountability costs. Preferences must be supplied and tested; the equation is not a disguised motive claim.
- **Latent-variable measurement model.** Y_{jct} = alpha_j + lambda_j H*_{ct} + gamma_j' X_{ct} + u_{jct}; H* unaccountable cross-domain concentration; measurement invariance must be tested across regimes; absence argues for regime-specific models.
- **Event-study specification.** Y_{cst} = mu_{cs} + tau_t + sum_{k != -1} beta_k D^{(k)}_{cs,t} + theta' X_{cst} + eps_{cst}; D^(k) time relative to an oversight reform; mu sector-jurisdiction FE; tau year FE; causal reading needs selection, parallel trends, no anticipation, no interference.

**Notation-clash warnings for Part VII (flagged by KB builder):** (a) B_t (dependency matrix), B(p) (coalition benefit) and the new "B(G)" predicate / "Proposition B" collide: use \mathsf{Struct}(G) for the structural predicate and keep Proposition A/B as prose labels; (b) the source's F is the role map but the series uses \mathsf{F} for the financial pillar; (c) I_t vs \mathsf{I}; (d) x_t uses I_t^cap.

### 2.6 Propositions (conditional, source Section 5.4)
- **Prop 1 (integration is not implied by differentiation).** Differentiated resource, legal, epistemic, enforcement and labor functions do not establish a common hierarchy; evidence of cross-functional command, privileged coordination or joint suppression of contestation is required. Follows from the possibility of independent and adversarial nodes in G_t.
- **Prop 2 (checks can interrupt transmission).** Holding resource concentration constant, effective independent review and voice should reduce the probability that attempted capture yields persistent unreviewable outcomes. Conditional comparative static, depends on checks actually binding.
- **Prop 3 (control paths differ by regime).** Private-economy electoral systems: concentrated economic resources -> lobbying and policy influence. Party-state command economies: central appointment authority -> allocation and enforcement. Both can yield dependency; direction and remedies differ. Rejects "identical apexes".
- **Prop 4 (public benefit is compatible with hierarchy).** Coordinated capacities can produce high welfare under transparent mandates and error correction; centralization correlated with service delivery would not falsify hierarchy but challenges equating hierarchy with exploitation. Theory must predict when centralization becomes arbitrary domination.
- **Counterexamples listed:** courts invalidating donor-backed policies; military refusing unlawful orders; professional bodies publishing findings unwelcome to funders; elected redistribution; unions winning without elite permission.

### 2.7 Hypotheses (source Section 9.3) with falsifiers
| ID | Statement | Falsifier stated by source |
|---|---|---|
| H1 capture and closure | Within comparable sectors, greater concentration of discretionary benefits among connected actors is associated with weaker effective challenge opportunities; weakens after independent adjudication or transparent competitive entry | insignificant or opposite pattern after credible measurement |
| H2 integration and durability | Organizations with repeated cross-domain control over appointments, funding and sanctions preserve preferential rules after adverse disclosure better than resource-rich organizations lacking integration | persistence of specified rules, not analyst description |
| H3 party-state pathway | In one-party command sectors appointment chains explain allocation and survival more than private capital shares; in some market sectors concentrated ownership explains more of policy access | enterprise autonomy, foreign investment or informal patronage changes the channel |
| H4 epistemic checks | More independently governed access to administrative and scientific data predicts higher rate of documented error discovery and policy correction (higher short-run discovery can signal stronger accountability) | no relation |
| H5 worker voice | Where workers have bargaining, legal protection, mobility and representation, concentration elsewhere translates less into material dependency | nominal vs practical access must be separated |
| H6 public goods as rival | Concentration with high-quality external review can coexist with competent services and low arbitrary sanctioning, weakening the claim that centralization is inherently oppressive | -- |

Four findings that would undermine the strong thesis (Section 9.5): disaggregated policy domains governed by unrelated actors with conflicting interests; accountability channels repeatedly reversing advantages; lower worker vulnerability with more public provision; political transitions producing durable shifts in allocation and legal authority.

### 2.8 Mechanisms A-E (Section 7), failure modes
| Mech | Content | Fails or weakens when |
|---|---|---|
| A resource dependence and selective rents | investment, employment, media reach, expertise give leverage; concentrated beneficiaries monitor rules (Olson) | open entry, transparent procurement, conflict disclosure, judicial and legislative scrutiny, antitrust |
| B lawmaking, administrative discretion, legitimacy | binding obligations; complexity favors well-resourced actors | legal predictability, due process, judicial independence bind rulers |
| C narratives, knowledge, professional authority | problem definition precedes solution choice (Bachrach-Baratz, Lukes, Scott); funding shapes questions; licensing | independent epidemiology, investigative reporting, protected academic freedom, professional resistance |
| D sanctions and credible threats | seizure, detention, fines, force; unreviewable surveillance and selective punishment | sanctioning bodies independent of reviewed actors; civilian oversight; complaints systems |
| E work, material flows, learned compliance | taxes, labor, care, consumption, socialization; dependence rises with no savings, employers, mobility, legal aid, union, voice | portable benefits, bargaining coverage, income protection, access to courts |

### 2.9 Comparative cases (Section 8)
1. **United States: organized interests and contestation.** Path: unequal private resources -> advocacy -> access -> specialized policy development -> distribution of legal benefits. Cites Stigler 1971, Gilens & Page 2014 (1,779 issues), OECD 2021 lobbying. Proposes narrow projects (procurement decisions, environmental exemptions over two decades) and pharma/insurer reimbursement conflicts. Countervailing evidence: competitive tendering won on merit, sanctions after disclosure, judicial invalidation, electoral policy change.
2. **Soviet party-state: appointments and bureaucratic bargaining.** Reverses the capital-first story: appointment hierarchies and central production decisions; Kornai soft budget constraint; Lane and Ross (1995) on CPSU elite divisions 1981-1991; informal exchange; test by tracing one strategic sector.
3. **Post-socialist transition: exchange between wealth and authority.** Hellman-Jones-Kaufmann state capture; privatization -> wealth concentration -> rule-making capacity -> protection of ownership; need matched sector comparisons.
4. **Cross-case result:** three pathways: wealth-to-rules, appointments-to-resources, reciprocal capture in transition.

### 2.10 Objections section (Section 10)
Pluralist (Dahl: no single apex; response network hierarchy vs single-actor monopoly, evidentiary burden); functionalist (concentration may be necessary, Mann infrastructural power); Marxian (class relations underdeveloped; response: mid-range institutional theory, W active); conservative/liberal (order requires authority; no generalized distrust); methodological (unfalsifiable "self-sealing"; register negative cases); ethical (professional categories are not moral identities; describe acts before charged symbols).

### 2.11 Redesign (Section 11) and appendices
11.1 procurement/beneficial-ownership transparency, lobbying registers (OECD); 11.2 independent courts, counsel, administrative appeal, whistleblower protection; 11.3 open knowledge: protocols, COI registries, negative-result publication; 11.4 civilian control and accountable security; 11.5 labor mobility, portable benefits, bargaining (Hirschman). Principle: separation of effective control with real contestation; decentralization alone can create fiefdoms. Appendix A data dictionary (country_period, sector_policy, actor_id, functional_roles, link_type, link_evidence, public_mandate, privileged_access, decision_outcome, independent_review, material_effect, counterevidence, coder_confidence). Appendix B preregistration protocol (population: all policy events in preregistered sectors, not only scandals; exposures; controls; inference; adversarial validation). Appendix C five decision questions and three reasoning errors (composition, post hoc causation, unfalsifiable coordination). Appendix D primary-source inventory (lobbying disclosures, procurement, rulemaking dockets, IG audits; party appointment lists, ministerial archives; V-Dem v16, WGI).

## 3. THEOLOGICAL READING (shared; owned by Part VII, used by all)

### 3.1 Text (KJV, public domain) and structure
- **Rev 13:1-10 first beast (from the sea):** authority (13:2 "the dragon gave him his power, and his seat, and great authority"), war-making and universal reach (13:4 "Who is like unto the beast? who is able to make war with him?"; 13:7 "power was given him over all kindreds, and tongues, and nations"), worship (13:8).
- **Rev 13:11-15 second beast (from the earth):** speaks, performs signs, makes an image and enforces worship of the first (13:12, 13:14-15). In the typological reading: the persuasion/legitimation function serving political power.
- **Rev 13:16-17:** "And he causeth all, both small and great, rich and poor, free and bond, to receive a mark in their right hand, or in their foreheads: And that no man might buy or sell, save he that had the mark, or the name of the beast, or the number of his name." Economic participation conditioned on allegiance.
- **Rev 17:** the great harlot "sitteth upon many waters" (17:1), explained as "peoples, and multitudes, and nations, and tongues" (17:15); ten kings give "their power and strength unto the beast" (17:13). Political coalition and delegated authority.
- **Rev 18:** Babylon's fall; kings of the earth, merchants and shipmasters lament (18:9-19); cargo list 18:11-13 ends with "slaves, and souls of men" (the Greek lists somaton kai psychas anthropon); the merchants "were the great men of the earth" (18:23); 18:4 "Come out of her, my people". Economic critique of imperial commerce.
- **Mapping to the six pillars (typological heuristic only, never an identification):** sea-beast authority and war = P + C; dragon/seat = legitimating source of authority (I); image-speaking second beast = I; mark/buy-and-sell = F + W coupled to allegiance (F gatekeeping, W compelled participation); Babylon's merchants = F; judgment/"true and righteous" (19:2) as the counter-image of J. Present this as an analogy aiding comparison, not as exegesis.

### 3.2 Interpretive schools (state which one you use)
| Reading | Claim | Use in series | Key sources |
|---|---|---|---|
| Preterist / contextual-historical | Beast = Roman imperial power (Nero/Domitian cycle); 666 as gematria of Nero Caesar (variant 616); imperial cult in Asia Minor | historical anchor for Rev 13 and 18 (Rome, Asian imperial cult, trade guilds) | bauckham1993, collins1984, aune1998b, koester2014, friesen2001, price1984, kraybill1996, thompson1990, hemer1986 |
| Typological / idealist | Beast = recurring pattern of power that claims ultimate loyalty; Babylon = the idolatrous city in every age | **the series' reading** | bauckham1993 (Rome as paradigm), beale1999, wink1992 (Domination System), ellul1977, rossing1999 |
| Futurist | Text predicts a specific future political-economic order and person | **excluded by guardrail 4**; note its reception history to show why identifications fail | boyer1992, fuller1995 (Naming the Antichrist), mcginn1998 |
| Historicist | Beast = e.g. the papacy (Reformation) | Document as history of reception; a reminder that identifications of Catholic or any institution with the beast are polemic and excluded | emmerson1992, mcginn1998, kovacs2004 |

Notes: Bauckham (1991, 1993) reads Rev 18 as an economic critique of Rome's trade empire with a prophetic function; Kraybill (1996) ties participation in guilds and imperial cult to commerce; Friesen (2001) uses archaeology of the Ephesian imperial cult; Wink treats "powers" as the inner spirituality of institutions (domination system), which supports a structural-universal reading; Aune WBC 52B/52C is the standard philological commentary. Alternative text-critical readings (e.g., 616 for 666) exist; do not use gematria to identify anyone.

**Reconciling typology with Proposition A:** Rev 13 describes a configuration (power + legitimation + economic gatekeeping), which the series generalizes as the six-function structure present in every polity. The normative warning attaches to Proposition B (when allegiance is made unconditional and unreviewable, cf. source Section 3), not to the existence of the functions. Pay attention: this is an interpretive claim of the project, not a settled exegetical result; cite it as such.

## 4. STRUCTURAL NECESSITY (for Part VII; Parts I-VI cite)

### 4.1 Statement
Let G range over functioning human governance systems (a collective of agents persisting over time that makes and enforces collective decisions). Proposition A: every G has an implemented realization of each of F, P, J, I, C, W together with dependency relations among them. Proposition B: U_t and I_t both high with capture links. A does not imply B.

### 4.2 Axioms (working set; Part VII refines and owns)
| Ax | Content | Yields | Informal minimal form |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ax1 scarcity | resources finite; claims conflict | F allocation | sharing norms, demand sharing, access rules |
| Ax2 plurality | preferences diverge; some collective choices must be made | P rule-making | consensus meetings, moots, camp-level decisions |
| Ax3 generality | rules general, cases particular, rules have open texture | J interpretation | elders and mediators; leopard-skin chief |
| Ax4 coordination | cooperation needs common knowledge of rules and expectations | I legitimation | myth, ritual, naming, ridicule |
| Ax5 defection | some members sometimes break rules | C sanction | gossip, shunning, ostracism, feud |
| Ax6 maintenance | the group and its institutions consume resources that someone produces | W | foraging, household labor |

### 4.3 Proof architecture (suggested for Part VII)
1. **Definitions.** A function m is *implemented* in G if some set of agents performs the activity-type that discharges the corresponding problem with non-trivial regularity; implementation may be fused (one agent several functions), distributed (many agents), or informal (no designated office). Define the role map surjectivity: for every m in M, the set {v : m in role(v)} is nonempty.
2. **Lemma per axiom (necessity, role-level not institution-level).** Given Axk and persistence of G, not-implemented(m_k) implies the corresponding problem is unresolved, which contradicts persistence or the axiom: e.g., not-C: defection with no sanction -> defection spreads (Hobbes covenant without sword; Fehr-Gachter 2002; Axelrod 1984 shows reciprocity itself is a sanction); not-J: general rules unapplied in disputed particular cases -> disputes unresolved (Hart's secondary rule of adjudication, open texture); not-P: no means to change or add rules -> static rules in changing environment (Hart's rule of change); not-I: no common knowledge of rules -> coordination failure (Lewis 1969, Chwe 2001, Aumann 1976; Hart's rule of recognition); not-F: unresolved conflict over finite resources; not-W: stock-consuming system is not persistent.
3. **Dependency relations (derived).** Propose directed dependencies (to be proved as lemmas, not asserted):
   - W -> F (production creates the stock to allocate); F -> W (allocation of means of production/maintenance sustains producers); F -> C (sanction capacity requires provisioning; Tilly; Levi); C -> F (enforcement of allocation rules, property); P -> J (rules to interpret); J -> P (interpretation feeds back, Hart's rule of change by precedent); I -> P, J, C (recognition of rules and officials: Hart; Weber legitimate domination); C -> J (adjudication without enforcement is advice); J -> I (reasoned judgments are public symbolic acts); W -> I (specialists in belief maintenance need surplus); F -> I (funding of ideology producers); I -> W (norms of work, compliance, taxpaying: Levi's quasi-voluntary compliance). That gives a candidate dependency digraph D on six nodes; the claim "dependency relations among the functions" in Proposition A is that D is strongly connected (each function is reachable from each other) in any G that satisfies Ax1-6. **Open problem for Part VII: give a proof that strong connectivity is forced rather than assumed.**
4. **Non-implication A -/-> B.** Construct two models over the same role-map structure. Model G1: six functions realized, each by many independent organizations, with contestability Q near 1 and C_{mn} near 0: U_t small, I_t small, A holds, B fails. Model G2: one organization realizes all six functions with Q near 0: U_t near maximal, I_t near 1: both A and B. Therefore A is independent of B. In the formal vocabulary: A is a statement about the *support* of the role map and the existence of dependencies; B is a statement about the *values* of (H, Q, C). Add a measure-theoretic remark: A is a qualitative predicate on the role map; B is a threshold on a real-valued functional.
5. **Conditional axioms for tendency toward B** (separate from A): (Michels-type) delegation cost: monitoring and replacement costs of leaders exceed members' benefits (Michels 1915; Olson 1965 organization asymmetry); (Mosca) organized minority beats unorganized majority; (Stigler/Peltzman) concentrated gains vs diffuse costs; (NWW) violence specialists take rents unless constrained. Checks that interrupt (source Prop 2): Madison Fed 51 structure of counter-ambition; Montesquieu; Hirschman exit-voice; Ostrom design principles (monitoring, graduated sanctions, conflict-resolution mechanisms); open entry; press; independent courts. Stress that Michels's law has documented exceptions (Lipset-Trow-Coleman 1956 ITU; Voss and Sherman 2000; Cassinelli 1953 critique), so tendency is not necessity.

### 4.4 Avoiding functionalist fallacies (mandatory for all Parts)
- **Hempel 1959:** from "function X is needed" it does not follow that institution Y exists (affirming the consequent); several functional equivalents may satisfy the need. Therefore claim necessity of the *role*, not of any particular *institution*; and state the minimal-form lemma.
- **Elster (1979, 1983, 1985):** a functional explanation of a trait by its beneficial effects is legitimate only with a feedback loop (selection mechanism) from effect to trait. Do not explain why a given regime has a given institution merely by its function. Proposition A is a *conditions-of-persistence* claim, not a causal-explanation claim.
- **Merton 1968:** distinguish manifest/latent functions and dysfunctions; functional alternatives. **Aberle et al. 1950** list functional prerequisites (provision for adequate relationship to environment and sexual recruitment, role differentiation and role assignment, communication, shared cognitive orientations, shared articulated goals, normative regulation of means, regulation of affective expression, socialization, effective control of disruptive forms of behavior): borrowed as a precedent, but their list is broader than six and was critiqued as tautological. **Parsons (1951) AGIL** (adaptation, goal attainment, integration, latency/pattern maintenance) is a four-function precedent; mapping: A~F/W, G~P(+C), I~J/C (integration of norms), L~I. **G. A. Cohen 1978** defends functional explanation in Marx; read with Elster.
- **Vacuity risk:** if "function" is defined so loosely that anything counts, A is trivial. Provide the falsifier: exhibit a persistent collective that satisfies Ax1-6 yet has no agent or practice performing one of the six activity types. Anthropological hard cases (Section 5) are the test set.
- **Axiom risk:** Ax1-6 are empirical-theoretical assumptions about collective life, not logical truths. State them as such and discuss which weakening (e.g., drop Ax5 for a population of perfectly cooperative agents) would remove a pillar. This is the honest content of "proof from minimal axioms".
- **Hart (1961):** primary rules (obligations) and secondary rules of change (~P), adjudication (~J), recognition (~I). Hart notes a regime of only primary rules is static, inefficient and uncertain: the three defects map exactly to the need for P, J, I. He treats a pre-legal community of primary rules as possible but defective. Confirm the page references in the 3rd edition ch. V before citing.
- **Hobbes (1651):** covenants without the sword are but words (ch. 17); defection motivates C.
- **Mann (1986, 1993):** IEMP networks (ideological, economic, military, political) as organizational means of power, overlapping, with no single prime mover; closest existing framework to the six pillars: Mann has no separate judicial or productive-population pillar, and his "political" source is the centralized territorial regulation. See Part VII table.

## 5. HARD CASES: STATELESS AND CONSENSUS SOCIETIES (every Part uses)

Purpose: test Proposition A against societies commonly described as having no state, no chiefs with coercive power, or radical egalitarianism. The thesis predicts each function in informal or distributed form. Be fair: for each, note what the source says, the **minimal form of each function**, and the **strongest reading that the function is absent**. Ethnographic caveats: contact-era ethnography, selection bias (observed groups are marginal environments), idealization, disputed generalization (Scott 2009 Zomia is not a universal).

| Case (key) | F allocation | P rule-making | J interpretation | I legitimation | C sanction | W production | Strongest counter-reading |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Nuer** (evanspritchard1940, fortes1940; "ordered anarchy"; Kuper 1982 critique of lineage models is worth checking) | cattle rights through kinship, bridewealth, grazing access, sharing of meat and milk | no legislature; norms altered through lineage moots and precedent of settled feuds; age-set initiation as organizing | leopard-skin chief (not a chief in a political sense) mediates blood-feud compensation (cattle payments); elders and prophets as arbitrators | segmentary lineage ideology, cosmology of Kwoth, ritual; prophets (ngun) | feud and balanced opposition, threat of retaliation by agnates | pastoralism, horticulture, fishing | Evans-Pritchard himself stressed "no government"; the leopard-skin chief has no coercive authority: decisions accepted by consent. Reply: J works through mediation + compensation backed by feud threat (C). Function present, office absent |
| **Tiv, Arusha, Barotse etc.** (gluckman1955, gulliver1963) | land tenure and allocation rules | council deliberation | moots, "reasonable man" standard (Gluckman) | custom, kinship ritual | ostracism, compensation, sorcery accusation | farming | These are chiefdoms or states in Gluckman's case; used as contrast |
| **Amazonian chiefs / Clastres** (clastres1987; Society Against the State) | chief obliged to be generous, gifts flow from chief to group (reverse of tribute) | chief has no power to command; speech-making as duty; group decisions by consensus | chief as peacemaker and orator | tribal mythology; the group's collective refusal of division between ruler and ruled | chief who attempts to command is abandoned, ridiculed or killed (Clastres reports); warfare keeps groups dispersed | gardening, hunting; chief works harder | Clastres argues these societies *actively prevent* the political function from separating from society. This is consistent with A (functions exist, unseparated) but contradicts any thesis that separation is inevitable. Critique: Clastres idealizes; evidence is selective (check Lizot, Chagnon debates; Graeber 2004 Fragments of an Anarchist Anthropology) |
| **Boehm: reverse dominance hierarchy** (boehm1999, boehm2012) | meat-sharing norms enforced against hoarders | egalitarian decision-making by coalition of subordinates; "they decide as a group" | group "talking out" disputes; third-party mediators | moral community, moral talk; gossip circulates norms | **gossip, ridicule, criticism, disobedience, ostracism, exile and execution of would-be alphas or deviants** (Boehm's cross-cultural sample of 48 foraging societies; confirm the number from the book before quoting) | foraging | Boehm's own conclusion supports A: egalitarianism is not absence of hierarchy but a politically active inversion (the group dominates the would-be dominant). Counter-reading: sample uses ethnographic reports of varied depth |
| **Ju/'hoansi (!Kung)** (lee1979, lee2013, marshall1976, wiessner2005) | strong sharing norms: meat distribution by arrow owner rule; hxaro gift exchange networks; demand sharing; "insulting the meat" levels hunters | camp-level talk; people vote with their feet (fission); n!ore territorial ownership by kin | disputes handled by talk, kin mediators; healing dances to defuse tension | n/um healing ritual, egalitarian ethos, myths | gossip, teasing, avoidance; in extreme cases killing (Lee's records of homicides); group-level collective action against persistent offenders (Wiessner 2005 on norm enforcement via talk) | foraging by both sexes; Lee: ~15-20 hours/week food getting (check) | the case most cited as "no hierarchy". Reply: no designated office, but all six activity types identifiable; the egalitarian ethos is itself sustained by leveling sanctions (Woodburn 1982) |
| **Woodburn: immediate-return egalitarian societies** (woodburn1982: Hadza, Mbuti, !Kung, Batek) | no storage, no accumulation, sharing is obligatory | consensus; deliberate avoidance of positions of authority | no formal adjudication; disputes handled by leaving | ritual (e.g. Hadza epeme) that is egalitarian; | "institutions of leveling" that prevent accumulation of power: sharing, mocking, avoidance of leaders | immediate return from the environment | Woodburn's concept shows that egalitarianism is an *institutionally produced* outcome with enforcement |
| **Zomia / state avoidance** (scott2009; scott2017) | swidden cultivation, escape crops (cassava) chosen to be non-appropriable | segmentary, anti-state structures | oral genealogy flexible | oral culture as strategy of historical flexibility | refuge by flight | upland farming | Scott's thesis: much "primitiveness" is a *choice* to avoid the state; but those societies are governed (informal), not ungoverned; Scott 2017: early states as grain-based extractive concentrations (taxable crops, labor, legibility) show *where* F, W concentrate |
| **The Dawn of Everything** (graeber2021) | seasonal flexibility between hierarchical and egalitarian modes | deliberate constitutional experimentation; "three freedoms" (to disobey, to leave, to reshape social relations) | ritual courts in Indigenous North America (e.g., Wendat council) | ritual and carnival seasons | seasonal police: temporary coercive authority in Plains buffalo hunt (cited by authors) | | Claims: early societies self-consciously chose different political forms; hierarchy is not inevitable. This strengthens the distinction between A (functions, universal) and B (concentration, contingent). Review debates: critics dispute selective evidence, so cite with the reviews (check) |

**Use in Parts:** each Part's "Necessity derivation" lists the case-specific minimal form and strongest counterexample attempt. The contrast between Clastres/Boehm/Woodburn (societies that *actively prevent* concentration) and Scott 2017 (grain states that *enable* concentration) gives the cleanest empirical evidence that B is contingent while A is universal.

**Caution about hunter-gatherer baselines:** see Kelly 1995 on foraging diversity (kelly1995); Sahlins 1972 (original affluent society) shows that scarcity claims should be phrased as conflicting claims, not absolute shortage; Service 1962 and Fried 1967 typologies (band-tribe-chiefdom-state) are widely criticized as unilineal, and Graeber and Wengrow explicitly reject them.

# 6. PART-BY-PART KNOWLEDGE (Sections 6-9 in the order I-VII below)


# PART I: FINANCIAL POWER (pillar \mathsf{F}; source function R). "Who controls the resources?"

## I.1 Role of this Part
Owns: concentration index H^F, resource-to-rule edges, rents, soft budget constraint, state capture (Hellman et al.), rentier state, fiscal-military state *as a resource-allocation object* (taxation as extraction is shared with Part VI; Part I owns the allocation side), financial intermediaries (banks, central banks, sovereign debt, waqf/zakat/bayt al-mal, papal finance).
Does not own: lobbying-as-political-process (Part II), compliance tax payers (Part VI).

## I.2 Necessity derivation (Ax1 scarcity => F)
- **Axiom yielding the pillar:** Ax1: resources are finite and claims on them conflict; some rule must determine who gets what, who may use what, and what is held in reserve. This is the allocation function F: control of production inputs, stores, credit/debt, tribute, public budgets.
- **Minimal informal form:** sharing norms and access rules in foraging bands (meat distribution by arrow-owner rule, hxaro exchange partnerships, demand sharing; leveling of boasting hunters: lee1979, lee2013, marshall1976); cattle rights and bridewealth among the Nuer (evanspritchard1940); chief's obligatory generosity in Clastres's Amazonian cases (clastres1987); Woodburn's immediate-return systems where obligatory sharing replaces accumulation (woodburn1982). Control share s^F_i is then highly dispersed (H^F near 1/n), but is not zero and not undefined.
- **Strongest counterexample attempt:** (a) "original affluent society" (sahlins1972b): wants are limited, so no allocation problem. Reply: scarcity in Ax1 is relative to claims, not absolute; even abundant groups have rules for who may take, store, and share particular goods (meat), and conflicts over claims (marshall1976, lee1979). (b) post-scarcity automation: not an existing governance system, and still allocates compute, land, energy, attention. (c) Pure market with no collective allocation: markets presuppose property rules (Part II/III) and a unit of account; allocation by price is still F, held by the pattern of endowments. **Falsifier:** a persistent collective with conflicting claims on any good but no norm or practice of access, sharing or exchange.
- **Note:** F is the pillar most often confused with "capitalists". Under the necessity reading F exists in a hunter-gatherer camp; what is contingent is H^F and Q^F.

## I.3 Key concepts and definitions (use these)
1. **Control share s^F_{i,t}** (notation.md): normalize decision-relevant control over resources: ownership, credit allocation authority, budget authority, procurement award authority, tribute/rent collection. Observables: asset shares, bank credit shares, SOE output shares, share of public budget under discretionary control. H^F_t = sum_i (s^F_{i,t})^2.
2. **Contestability Q^F:** open entry, competitive tendering, beneficial-ownership disclosure, independent audit, bankruptcy that actually bites (hard budget constraint), antitrust, central bank independence.
3. **Soft budget constraint** (kornai1986, kornai1992): enterprise expects rescue so budget discipline fails; found in socialist firms, but also banks "too big to fail" (generalization requires care; source cites Kornai only for socialism).
4. **Rent seeking and capture:** Krueger 1974, Tullock 1967, Posner 1975, Stigler 1971, Peltzman 1976, Becker 1983 (rival: competition among pressure groups limits deadweight loss), Laffont-Tirole 1991, Dal Bo 2006, Carpenter-Moss 2014.
5. **State capture** (Hellman et al. 2000; hellman1998): firms shape the rules themselves. Distinguish *influence* (legal access) from *capture link* (source definition requiring privileged discretion, weakened accountability, and evidence tied to output).
6. **Limited vs open access orders** (nww2009): rents allocated to elites to buy peace; open access = open entry to economic and political organizations.
7. **Rentier state** (mahdavy1970, beblawiluciani1987, ross2001, ross2012, chaudhry1997, hertog2010). Counter-evidence: habermenaldo2011.
8. **Fiscal-military state and sovereign borrowing** (drelichman2014, conklin1998, velde1992, karaman2013): lenders and rulers in repeated games.
9. **Institutional conversion rate:** the elasticity of policy to resource advantage; Bourdieu 1989 convertibility of capitals (economic <-> political <-> symbolic); Walzer 1983 "tyranny" as conversion of dominance in one sphere into another (that is exactly cross-pillar C_{FP}).
10. **Cross-pillar integration with F:** C_{FP} (campaign money, revolving door, party-controlled appointment of managers), C_{FC} (military-industrial interlock, party-state fiscal control of security forces, IRGC business activity), C_{FI} (sponsorship of research and media), C_{FW} (employer concentration, company towns), C_{FJ} (litigation resources, repeat players: Galanter 1974, see Part III).

## I.4 Six-regime comparison table (who holds F, how coupled, what keeps it accountable)
| Regime | Who holds F (typical) | Illustration 1 | Illustration 2 | Accountability / capture-interrupting factors | Key refs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Market-capitalist | Private owners, banks, corporations, institutional investors; central bank | US financial-sector lobbying before 2008: lenders that lobbied more engaged in riskier lending; contributions and votes on mortgage legislation | Politically connected firms cross-country: share of connected firms and valuation effects (Faccio); Suharto-connected firm value (Fisman); Pakistan connected-borrower lending (Khwaja-Mian) | antitrust, disclosure, open entry; but Ansolabehere et al. find little money in US politics relative to stakes (rival: contributions are consumption); Baumgartner et al. find money advantage rarely decides; Gilens-Page vs Branham et al. | igan2012, mian2010, faccio2006, fisman2001, khwaja2005, ansolabehere2003, baumgartner2009, gilens2014, branham2017, hackerpierson2010, bartels2008 |
| 2 Communist party-state | Party-state planners and appointed managers; state banks; SOEs | Soviet planning: priorities, tolkachi, blat; soft budget constraint; archival reconstruction of top-level allocation | PRC: state-dominated banking and SOEs coexisting with a large private sector; party appointment of SOE heads; "state capitalism" ownership is weak proxy | party appointment lists (nomenklatura) as the allocation channel (source H3); informal exchange; hard constraints from reform | gregory2004, berliner1957, ledeneva1998, kornai1992, allen2005, walterhowie2011, lardy2014, milhaupt2015, naughton2007 |
| 3 Democratic/market socialism, social democracy | Mixed: private capital under high-tax and corporatist bargains; public pension and wage-earner funds; public banks | Sweden: wage-earner fund proposals (Meidner) as labor-movement attempt to share capital control; concentrated family ownership (Wallenberg sphere) coexisting with high taxation | Coordinated market economies (Hall-Soskice); Streeck's argument on creditors vs citizens; Przeworski's capital-dependence dilemma | collective bargaining, generous welfare reduce dependence (but capital strike threat remains); transparency norms | pontusson1992, korpi1983, esping1985, hogfeldt2005, hallsoskice2001, przeworski1985, streeck2014, berman2006 |
| 4 Islamic states | Ruler/state treasury (bayt al-mal), waqf endowments under jurists/trustees, merchants (qirad/mudaraba partnerships), tax farmers (Ottoman iltizam/malikane), oil-rent states | Waqf and zakat: classical charitable endowments providing public goods, partly insulated from rulers (contested); Ottoman state finances, tax farming and fiscal capacity | Rentier monarchies and republics: Saudi allocation (Hertog), Iran's bonyads and IRGC business interests (Maloney, Wehrey et al.) | variation: Ottoman waqf supervised by qadis; modern oil states: rents reduce tax bargaining (Ross) but contested (Haber-Menaldo) | kuran2001, singer2008, kuran2004, kuran2011 (contested; pair with critics), karaman2010, pamuk2000, cizakca1996, udovitch1970, hertog2010, chaudhry1997, maloney2015, wehrey2009, ross2001, elgamal2006 |
| 5 Historical Catholic monarchies | Crown fiscal apparatus; private bankers (Fugger, Genoese asientos, Medici); Church property, tithes, papal curia finance; venal officeholders | Habsburg Spain: Genoese and German bankers; repeated defaults ("bankruptcies") yet continued lending; Fugger/Medici as crown/papal financiers | Bourbon France: venal offices, tax farming (fermiers generaux), rentes; privilege as fiscal sorting | Cortes/Parlements constraints; bankers' repeated-game discipline; ecclesiastical wealth immunities | drelichman2011, drelichman2014, conklin1998, padgett1993, deroover1963, prodi1987, ekelund1996, velde1992, kwass2000, doyle1996, hoffmannorberg1994, hoffman2000 |
| 6 Secular democracies | Same as 1 and 3 with stronger constitutional separation: independent central banks, audit offices, competitive procurement | Central bank independence as delegated financial authority (Alesina-Summers; Goodhart) | Transparent procurement, lobbying registers and beneficial-ownership registers (OECD 2021, in source); revolving door evidence | the question is whether delegation reduces or relocates concentration: independence reduces political capture but concentrates technocratic power | alesina1993, goodhart1988, blanes2012, bertrand2014, hall2006, kalla2016, carpentermoss2014, scheve2016 |
Note: regimes 1, 3 and 6 overlap analytically (secular democracies include market-capitalist and social-democratic ones). Treat 6 as the *constitutional design* dimension (separation, delegated independent bodies), 1 and 3 as the *economic organization* dimension. Make the overlap explicit and code cases on both dimensions.

## I.5 Candidate hypotheses (each with falsifier)
- **HF1 (conversion elasticity).** The elasticity of policy outcomes to concentrated-donor preference is higher for low-salience, technical issues (Culpepper 2010) than for high-salience ones. *Falsifier:* equal or higher elasticity at high salience; or elasticity vanishing once preference correlation among income groups is controlled (Branham et al.).
- **HF2 (party-state channel, source H3 specialised).** In party-state command sectors, appointment rank of the enterprise head predicts credit and input allocation after controlling for profitability and plan targets; in market sectors ownership share predicts policy access. *Falsifier:* profitability dominates rank.
- **HF3 (repeated-game discipline).** Where a ruler needs repeated access to credit, lenders obtain secure repayment without direct capture of legislation; default episodes are followed by renegotiated contracts rather than expropriation (Drelichman-Voth). *Falsifier:* lenders obtain legal advantages unrelated to borrowing needs.
- **HF4 (open entry).** Entry liberalization reduces rents of incumbent connected firms (Rajan-Zingales: incumbents oppose financial development when it threatens them). *Falsifier:* rents persist after entry reform.
- **HF5 (rentier effect, source mechanism A).** Higher non-tax revenue share lowers the rate of tax bargaining and increases discretionary allocation (Ross). *Falsifier:* within-country panel null (Haber-Menaldo).
- **HF6 (fused institutions).** Where a single organization holds F and C (e.g., a security force with business arms), Q^F falls and C_{FC} rises; this is the Iranian IRGC and PRC SOE-army cases to test; rival: public-goods provision by the same bodies (reconstruction, infrastructure).
- **HF7 (hard budget constraint as check).** Credible bankruptcy and exit of failing large firms is associated with lower rents (Kornai); *falsifier:* bailouts correlated with lower, not higher, subsequent rent.

## I.6 Measurement notes
- Observables for s^F: asset-share databases, bank credit shares, SOE share of output, procurement award shares (HHI by awardee), beneficial ownership registers; for Q^F: audit independence, procurement openness indexes, V-Dem corruption and judicial-constraint indicators, WGI Control of Corruption (kaufmann2010; coppedge2011).
- Use *capture-link coding* (Appendix A data dictionary): e.g., connected-firm exemption dates, link_evidence, independent_review.
- Rival explanations: efficiency of scale, natural monopoly, public-goods provision (source Prop 4/H6), survivorship (dynamic firms become politically connected because large).

## I.7 Gaps requiring web research
1. Chinese state-owned bank governance post-2015 (primary data from CBIRC/NFRA; Lardy 2019 The State Strikes Back is a candidate: check).
2. Fugger and papal finance primary-scholarship: Ehrenberg 1896 (Zeitalter der Fugger) and later Hildebrandt; Italian scholarship on the Camera Apostolica (Partner; Delumeau 1957-59 Vie economique et sociale de Rome).
3. Primary evidence on waqf expropriation (e.g., Ottoman 19th century nationalization; Egyptian Awqaf Ministry), modern zakat administration (Saudi, Iran, Pakistan).
4. Social-democratic financial structures beyond Sweden (Norwegian sovereign fund as public capital with ethical council; Danish pension funds).
5. The literature on "financial dominance"/fiscal dominance over central banks.
6. For Part I-VII integration: measures of C_{FP} (revolving door networks) with public data (LittleSis, OpenSecrets).

# PART II: POLITICAL POWER (pillar \mathsf{P}; source function L, rule-making half). "Who makes the laws?"

## II.1 Role of this Part
Owns: rule-making/legislation/agenda control, selection and succession of rulers, nomenklatura-type appointment, selectorate/winning coalition, party organization, oligarchy (Michels), pluralism (Dahl), power-sharing, legitimacy types (Weber) *as they bear on who makes rules*; regime typologies (Geddes; Lührmann et al.). Cross-pillar objects owned here: **appointment channel** (shared with J, C), **agenda control / non-decision** (Bachrach-Baratz, Lukes: shared with I), **veto players** (Tsebelis). Part II's U/Q: Q^P = electoral replacement, legislative oversight, free party formation, transparency of rule-making.
Boundary with III: P *makes or changes* general rules; J *applies and interprets* them. Boundary with V: control of the appointment of security chiefs is P; the sanctioning apparatus is V's.

## II.2 Necessity derivation (Ax2 plurality => P)
- **Axiom:** Ax2: members' preferences diverge, yet collective choices (when to move camp, who may marry whom, how to divide a kill, whether to go to war) must be made; there must be a procedure by which the group's rules are made, changed and set. Hart (1961): a regime of primary rules alone is *static*; it needs a "rule of change".
- **Minimal informal form:** camp-level talking and consensus among the Ju/'hoansi with fission as exit (lee2013); lineage and age-set moots among the Nuer (evanspritchard1940); Clastres's chief whose only power is oratory (clastres1987); Boehm's coalition of subordinates that decides collectively (boehm1999). Control share s^P is spread across all adult members (H^P ~ 1/n) with extremely high Q^P (exit and voice cheap).
- **Strongest counterexample attempt:** rules arising by evolved custom without any rule-making body (Hayek's spontaneous order: hayek1973; Ellickson's Shasta County "order without law": ellickson1991). Reply: Hart's rule of change can be diffuse and procedural (repeated practice, imitation, social pressure); the function of changing rules is performed, though not by a legislature; Ellickson's neighbors change norms via social-control networks, whose members are P-holders in the minimal sense. Honest weakness: this makes P a thin predicate; Part II must say what it excludes. **Falsifier:** a long-lived group whose rules never change and for which no agent can change them, in a changing environment (Hart's "static" defect), with persistent group survival.
- **Dependency note:** P needs I (recognition: who counts as a rule-maker; Hart's rule of recognition) and J (applications generate precedent that modifies rules) and C (rules without sanctions are advice).

## II.3 Key concepts
1. **Legitimate domination** (weber1978; weber1946): traditional, charismatic, rational-legal; monopoly claim on legitimate force shared with Part V. Series reading: legitimacy sources differ by regime (divine right, party vanguard, popular sovereignty, caliphal/imamate law).
2. **Iron law of oligarchy** (michels1915): organization -> delegation -> leadership entrenchment through expertise, control of communication, followers' apathy and gratitude. Critics/boundary: lipset1956 (ITU), voss2000, cassinelli1953. Conditional axiom for Prop B (tendency), not for Prop A.
3. **Pluralism and polyarchy** (dahl1961; dahl1971): contestation and inclusiveness; rival to elite theory.
4. **Two/three faces of power** (bachrach1962; lukes2005): decision, non-decision, preference shaping.
5. **Selectorate theory** (bdm2003): leader survival depends on winning coalition W within selectorate S; large W -> public goods, small W -> private goods. Provides a measurable alternative to H^P.
6. **Authoritarian power-sharing and control** (svolik2012): moral hazard of power-sharing among elites and adverse selection in control of the masses; institutionalization as solution; gandhi2007 and magaloni2008 on parties/legislatures; geddes2014 on regime types; hale2014 on patronal pyramids; levitsky2010 on competitive authoritarianism; gerschewski2013 three pillars.
7. **Nomenklatura** (voslensky1984, djilas1957; rigby/hough1979, fainsod1953): party control of appointments as the channel from P into F, I, J, C. Source Prop 3: appointment -> allocation/enforcement. Voslensky/Djilas are insider/dissident accounts: treat as sources with perspective, pair with Hough-Fainsod and Lane-Ross (lane1995) who find divisions.
8. **Veto players, delegation** (tsebelis2002; lijphart1999; stonesweet2000) and **separation of powers** (montesquieu1989; madison1788 Fed 51).
9. **Sovereignty and sacral kingship** (bodin1992; bossuet1990; figgis1914; kantorowicz1957): the Catholic-monarchy legitimating theory; Gallican limits (vankley1996).
10. **Caliphate and imamate theory** (mawardi1996; crone2004; crone1986; lambton1981; hallaq2013): the caliph/imam as executor of divine law, with jurists (ulama) as interpreters; al-Mawardi's *al-Ahkam al-Sultaniyya* lists conditions of the imamate and powers of viziers, governors, judges and others. Diversity: Sunni theory of necessity (al-Mawardi, al-Ghazali's later pragmatism), Shia imamate doctrine and **velayat-e faqih** (Khomeini 1970; Iranian 1979 constitution arts. 5, 107-112: Leader, Assembly of Experts; check article numbers), Saudi Basic Law of 1992 (Quran and Sunna as constitution; royal decrees; check text). Hallaq's thesis that the modern "Islamic state" is a contradiction in terms is one position; internal debate.
11. **Succession and selection:** hereditary monarchy (Habsburg, Bourbon), papal election (conclave), caliphal succession crises, party congress/Politburo, elections.

## II.4 Six-regime comparison table
| Regime | Who makes rules | Illustration 1 | Illustration 2 | Checks / accountability | Key refs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Market-capitalist | Elected legislatures + executive rule-making + agencies; organized interests shape drafts | US: policy responsiveness to economic elites and business groups vs average citizens (contested); lobbying as legislative subsidy | Revolving door: lobbyists' value tied to office connections; low-salience business power in Europe/Japan | elections, courts, press, antitrust; veto points can block as well as enable | gilens2014, branham2017, hall2006, blanes2012, bertrand2014, culpepper2010, bartels2008, hackerpierson2010, lindblom1977, mills1956, dahl1961 |
| 2 Communist party-state | Ruling party's organs (Politburo, Central Committee, congress), state legislature as ratifier | Soviet party-state: nomenklatura appointments, ministerial bargaining, elite divisions 1981-91 | PRC: single-party authoritarian institutions, Leninist party organization, promotion and cadre evaluation (check for citable PRC cadre system scholarship) | intra-party factionalism; term limits; reform; collapse risk (Svolik) | voslensky1984, djilas1957, hough1979, fainsod1953, lane1995, svolik2012, gandhi2007, shirk (gap), hale2014 |
| 3 Democratic socialism / social democracy | Parliaments with strong parties; corporatist bargaining with labor and employers | Swedish/Nordic corporatism and party-labor ties (power resources) | Social-democratic party strategy and electoral coalitions | competitive elections; interest-group access formalized (tripartite) which can itself be a closure risk | korpi1983, esping1985, przeworski1985, kitschelt1994, berman2006, lijphart1999, huber2001, rothstein1996 |
| 4 Islamic states | caliph/sultan with ulama as law-interpreters; modern: Leader + clerical councils (Iran), king + royal decrees + religious establishment (Saudi), parliaments in others | Classical caliphate/sultanate theory and practice: al-Mawardi's imamate vs actual military rulers; Ottoman sultanic qanun vs sharia | Iran: velayat-e faqih, Assembly of Experts, Guardian Council vetting; Saudi: Al Saud-Wahhabi pact | internal diversity; ulama independent authority vs co-optation; bay'a; elections in Iran (restricted) | mawardi1996, crone2004, crone1986, lambton1981, lewis1988, arjomand1988, khomeini1981, brumberg2001, buchta2000, alrasheed2002, commins2006, hegghammer2010, barkey2008, imber1997 |
| 5 Historical Catholic monarchies | Crown with councils, ministers/validos, estates (Cortes, Parlement/Estates-General), papal curia | Habsburg Spain: composite monarchy, councils (polysynody), Olivares's reform; Philip II's government | Bourbon France: absolutism as brokered with provincial elites (Beik); Richelieu; Louis XIV; Papal State as elective monarchy with nepotism and congregations | Cortes, Parlements, provincial estates, the Church's autonomous canon law; Gallican liberties vs ultramontane | bodin1992, bossuet1990, figgis1914, kantorowicz1957, elliott1963, elliott1984, beik1985, collins1995, prodi1987, vankley1996 |
| 6 Secular democracies | Elected legislatures; constitutional courts; independent agencies; direct democracy in some | Veto-player and consensus/majoritarian variation across democracies | Contestation and polyarchy indicators; backsliding (competitive authoritarianism boundary) | elections, opposition rights, constitutional review, free press | dahl1971, lijphart1999, tsebelis2002, linz1990, levitsky2010, coppedge2011, luhrmann2018, przeworski1991 |
(Entry "shirk (gap)": Shirk 1993 The Political Logic of Economic Reform in China is a recommended source but I did not verify it; add after web check.)

## II.5 Candidate hypotheses
- **HP1 (appointment channel).** In party-state regimes, the share of key economic, judicial, media and security posts filled by nomenklatura-listed appointees predicts C_{PF}, C_{PJ}, C_{PI}, C_{PC}; in secular democracies those pairwise integrations are lower because appointments are plural (legislative confirmation, civil-service rules). *Falsifier:* equal integration despite different appointment rules.
- **HP2 (power-sharing institutionalization).** Authoritarian regimes with functioning parties/legislatures have longer-lived leaders (Gandhi-Przeworski; Magaloni). *Falsifier:* no survival difference once resource rents are controlled.
- **HP3 (Michels conditional).** Leadership turnover in membership organizations is lower where monitoring cost is high and exit cheap is low; internal opposition institutions (e.g., ITU) reduce oligarchization (Lipset et al.). *Falsifier:* turnover independent of monitoring costs.
- **HP4 (selectorate).** Smaller winning coalitions predict higher private-goods spending (bdm2003). *Falsifier:* no association; or reverse.
- **HP5 (religious-political bargain).** Where rulers need religious legitimation, rulers tolerate religious authorities' autonomy in law and education in return for support (Rubin 2017, Kuru 2019 argue different directions; test both).
- **HP6 (divine-right absolutism is brokered).** French absolutism relied on negotiated intermediaries (Beik); test with variation in provincial estate survival.
- **HP7 (checks interrupt, source Prop 2).** Number of effective veto players is negatively associated with persistent preferential rules *and* with policy responsiveness (a trade-off; Tsebelis). *Falsifier:* no association with persistence of preferential rules.

## II.6 Measurement
V-Dem (coppedge2011; luhrmann2018) indicators: electoral democracy, legislative constraints on executive, judicial constraints, party institutionalization; Geddes regime dataset (geddes2014); selectorate data (bdm2003). For s^P: share of legislative proposals originating with an actor; share of appointments controlled by a coalition; number of effective veto players. Beware measurement invariance across regimes (source Section 9.2).

## II.7 Gaps requiring web research
1. PRC cadre management and party-state scholarship (Shirk 1993; Manion; Landry 2008 Decentralized Authoritarianism in China; Kroeber; Pei): choose verified entries.
2. Habsburg composite monarchy: Elliott 1992 "A Europe of Composite Monarchies" Past & Present 137; polysynody (Kamen).
3. Primary texts for Safavid/Ottoman ulama-state relations; Shia quietism vs activism; the 1979 constitution's articles 5, 107-112 (get an authoritative English translation, e.g., Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Iran, constituteproject.org).
4. Saudi Basic Law of Governance 1992 (primary).
5. Social-democratic parties: corporatism literature (Schmitter, Lehmbruch; Katzenstein 1985 Small States in World Markets).
6. Papal elections, conclave rules, nepotism (Reinhard); Papal bulls (Unam Sanctam) for the doctrine of two swords.

# PART III: JUDICIAL POWER (pillar \mathsf{J}; source function L, adjudication half). "Who interprets the laws?"

## III.1 Role
Owns: adjudication, dispute resolution, judicial review, judicial independence (de jure vs de facto), rule of law vs rule by law, legal profession, ecclesiastical/qadi/party courts as variants, legal pluralism. Shared objects it should cite from others: appointment channel (Part II); sanction/enforcement of judgments (Part V); repeat-player advantage (touches F). Q^J operationalization: appointment independence, tenure security, access (cost, counsel), appeal, reasoned decisions, compliance by the powerful.
Boundary with II: P writes rules; J says what they mean in the particular case. Source note: the source's L includes both; the series splits them. Where one body does both (a king-in-council, a Leader's council, a legislature acting as court), record **fusion**: C_{PJ} high.

## III.2 Necessity derivation (Ax3 generality => J)
- **Axiom:** Ax3: rules are general, cases particular; every rule has an "open texture" (Hart 1961) so application to a specific dispute needs a determination. Disputes about whether a rule was broken or what it requires arise among parties who will not all accept each other's reading. A third-party determination, or a procedure that yields one, is required to resolve it without perpetual feud.
- **Minimal informal form:** Nuer leopard-skin chief mediating blood-feud compensation by consent of the parties (evanspritchard1940); Barotse moots with the "reasonable man" standard (gluckman1955); Arusha dispute settlement through councils of elders (gulliver1963); Ju/'hoansi kin mediators and talking-out (lee2013); Boehm's group "talking out" (boehm1999). Shapiro (1981) argues the triad (two parties and a third who decides) is the universal prototype of courts (shapiro1981).
- **Strongest counterexample attempt:** (a) rule-systems without discretion: "code is law", self-executing contracts, algorithmic adjudication. Reply: ambiguity of inputs and unforeseen cases return the open-texture problem; someone (a developer, oracle, arbiter) resolves them: J relocates. (b) Societies resolving disputes by exit or fission only (Ju/'hoansi walk away): exit is a way to avoid adjudication, and where costs of exit are high a third party appears. (c) Pure feud/self-help (Evans-Pritchard's balanced opposition) with no third party: but compensation and oath-taking are adjudicative procedures. **Falsifier:** a persistent collective in which disputes about rule application are always settled by force of the parties with no third-party or procedural resolution, without the collective dissolving.
- **Dependency note:** J without C is advice (Hobbes); J without I lacks recognition; J feeds P by precedent (Hart's rule of change).

## III.3 Key concepts
1. **Triadic dispute resolution and courts as political actors** (shapiro1981).
2. **Rule of law, thin vs thick; rule by law** (tamanaha2004; tamanaha2006; peerenboom2002): rule by law = law as instrument of rulers; rule of law = rulers bound by law. Series: Q^J measures how far rulers are bound.
3. **Authoritarian courts: functions** (ginsburg2008; moustafa2007): social control, legitimation, credible commitment to investors, administrative discipline, controlling lower officials.
4. **Why rulers delegate to independent courts** (landes1975; ramseyer1994; stephenson2003; whittington2005; helmke2005): insurance for political losers; fragmented politics; but strategic defection when governments change (helmke2005).
5. **Hegemonic preservation / juristocracy** (hirschl2004; hirschl2000): elites entrench preferences in constitutional courts when threatened electorally; contested. **Constitutional theocracy** (hirschl2010): constitutionalization of religion and state (Iran's Guardian Council vetting legislation for sharia compatibility, Egypt's Supreme Constitutional Court, Pakistan's Federal Shariat Court; verify details).
6. **Judicial independence measurement** (laporta2004; feld2003): de jure vs de facto; tenure, appointment, case assignment.
7. **Legal mobilization and repeat players** (galanter1974; epp1998): structural advantages of organized repeat litigants: bridge to F (resources) and W (access for workers).
8. **Legal pluralism and religious courts**: qadi courts, muftis and fatwas (hallaq2009; hallaq2005; masud1996; messick1993; gerber1994; jennings1978; imber1997; zubaida2003; schacht1964 read critically); canon law and ecclesiastical courts (brundage1995; berman1983; helmholz1996); Inquisition (kamen1997; henningsen1986; given1997); papal justice and petitioning (fosi2011); French parlements and venality (bell2004; swann2003; doyle1996; also Mousnier on venality: gap); Castilian chanceries and litigiousness (kagan1981).
9. **Socialist legality** (berman1963; solomon1996; huskey1986; hendley1996; hendley2017; lubman1999; peerenboom2002; minzner2011): party control of courts via appointment and "telephone law" (check term source: Ledeneva 2006 How Russia Really Works), the "dual state" notion (Fraenkel 1941 for Nazi Germany; used for Russia by Hendley 2017 as dual: ordinary commercial cases vs political cases).
10. **Weak-form vs strong-form review** (tushnet2008), **constitutional courts as co-legislators** (stonesweet2000), **public support as shield** (vanberg2005; gibson2003).

## III.4 Six-regime comparison table
| Regime | Who interprets | Illustration 1 | Illustration 2 | Accountability / capture-interrupting | Key refs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Market-capitalist | Independent judiciaries; judges appointed (US: presidential nomination, Senate confirmation; state elections in some); a bar with large-firm advantage | US judicial review since Marbury v. Madison, 5 U.S. 137 (1803); judicial selection and ideological scaling of judges and lawyers | Repeat-player advantage in civil litigation (Galanter); legal mobilization by organized interests | tenure, appeal, public support, ethics rules; Hamilton's "least dangerous branch" (Fed 78) and its limits | hamilton1788, madison1788, whittington2005, bonica2017, galanter1974, epp1998, gibson2003, shapiro1981 |
| 2 Communist party-state | Courts under party committee (political-legal committee) control; procuracy; adjudication committees; advocates under state discipline | Soviet courts and procuracy in the Stalin and later periods: telephone justice, show trials as political events; advocates' bar | PRC: party leadership of courts; pragmatic commercial adjudication coexisting with political case control; "turn against law" after mid-2000s (check Minzner) | Internal professionalization pressures; occasional commercial independence; collapse of 1980s-90s Soviet legal reform | berman1963, solomon1996, huskey1986, hendley1996, hendley2017, peerenboom2002, lubman1999, minzner2011, ginsburg2008 |
| 3 Democratic / market socialism, social democracy | Courts with weaker or more restrained constitutional review in several Nordic systems; strong administrative courts and ombudsmen; German-type constitutional courts in social-market democracies | Tushnet's weak-form review models and social rights | German Federal Constitutional Court as active co-legislator in a social-market state | ombudsman, parliamentary scrutiny, judicial restraint; risk: legislative supremacy without independent check | tushnet2008, stonesweet2000, vanberg2005, hirschl2004 |
| 4 Islamic states | Qadis (state-appointed), jurists/muftis (independent scholarly authority), sultanic qanun courts (Ottoman), modern: codified state courts + sharia courts; Iran's Guardian Council; Saudi courts applying uncodified sharia under royal decrees | Ottoman kadi courts: accessible, records of ordinary petitioners (Kayseri); sultanic law and Seyhulislam fatwas (Ebussuud) | Iran: Guardian Council vetting of laws and candidates under velayat-e faqih; Saudi: courts and royal decrees (nizam) | juristic independence of ulama historically (Hallaq); modern state incorporation/co-optation; internal diversity (schools, Shia usuli) | hallaq2009, hallaq2005, jennings1978, gerber1994, imber1997, masud1996, messick1993, schirazi1997, hirschl2010, vogel2000, brown1997, zubaida2003 |
| 5 Historical Catholic monarchies | King's courts, Parlements/Audiencias/Chancillerias, ecclesiastical courts under canon law, Inquisition, Roman Rota/curial tribunals in the Papal State | Canon-law courts and the "Papal Revolution" origin of Western legal science; papal justice and petitioning in the Papal State | Spanish Inquisition (Kamen revisionism) and French Parlements (venal offices, remonstrance) | venal office-holding as property protection of judges; appeals to crown; jurisdictional conflict between Church and Crown | brundage1995, berman1983, helmholz1996, fosi2011, kamen1997, henningsen1986, given1997, bell2004, swann2003, doyle1996, kagan1981 |
| 6 Secular democracies | Constitutional/supreme courts, ordinary courts, administrative tribunals; international courts | Hirschl's hegemonic preservation cases (Israel, Canada, NZ, South Africa) | Constitutional courts as co-legislators (Germany, France Conseil constitutionnel); public support as shield | transparency, appeal, independent judicial councils; legislative court-curbing risk | hirschl2004, hirschl2000, stonesweet2000, vanberg2005, stephenson2003, ramseyer1994, laporta2004, feld2003 |

## III.5 Candidate hypotheses
- **HJ1 (insurance).** Judicial independence is greater where political power is more fragmented or electorally uncertain (Ramseyer; Stephenson; Whittington). *Falsifier:* independence unrelated to electoral competition across a country panel (use laporta2004/feld2003 or V-Dem judicial constraints).
- **HJ2 (hegemonic preservation).** Constitutional court empowerment follows declining electoral prospects of the hegemonic group (Hirschl). *Falsifier:* timing unrelated to electoral decline (Hirschl's critics, e.g., on Israel, Canada).
- **HJ3 (authoritarian court function).** Commercial adjudication is more independent than political adjudication in party-states (Hendley; Ginsburg-Moustafa). *Falsifier:* equal capture across case types.
- **HJ4 (repeat players).** Success rates of repeat litigants exceed one-shotters in comparable cases; reforms of legal aid reduce the gap (Galanter). *Falsifier:* no gap after case-merit controls.
- **HJ5 (religious-court autonomy).** Where the legal scholars (ulama) hold independent interpretive authority and are not salaried by the state, rulers' legislative discretion is limited to administrative regulation (Hallaq's thesis); under state incorporation of ulama, discretionary law grows. *Falsifier:* no difference between periods or states.
- **HJ6 (venality).** Venal judicial offices (France) protect judges from the crown (property in office) but entrench corporate privilege (Doyle; Bell). Test through remonstrance frequency and crown override (lits de justice).
- **HJ7 (fusion and capture).** C_{PJ} (appointment overlap) and U^J increase together when judges are appointed by and removable by the executive/party; but judges' reputational concerns may bound this (Helmke).
- **HJ8 (Prop 2 test).** Judicial review effectiveness (compliance rate of powerful agencies with adverse rulings) predicts whether capture links persist after disclosure (source H1/H2).

## III.6 Measurement
V-Dem judicial constraints on the executive, compliance with judiciary, high court independence; WGI Rule of Law (kaufmann2010); case-processing time; appeal success rates; share of rulings against government; access indicators (legal aid coverage). Distinguish formal guarantees from observed functioning (source Section 9.2). For historical regimes: archival court records (Kayseri court registers, parlement registers, Inquisition trial records), with selection caveats (henningsen1986).

## III.7 Gaps requiring web research
1. Primary text: Federalist No. 78 (URL verified if the status below says so), Marbury v. Madison, Citizens United (558 U.S. 310) for judicial doctrine on money and speech.
2. Iranian Guardian Council functions (articles 91-99 of the 1979 constitution; check), Judiciary head appointment by Leader (art. 157; check); Saudi Basic Law arts.
3. Mousnier on venality; Bluche on parlementaires; Castilian letrados (Kagan); Inquisition quantitative data (Contreras and Henningsen 1986 in Henningsen-Tedeschi).
4. Soviet law: Hazard; Feldbrugge; Kucherov; Stalin-era show trials (Rittersporn).
5. Nordic judicial review: Sweden's Instrument of Government (manifest error test), Finland's constitutional committee; Danish Supreme Court Ajos case. Needs verified scholarship.
6. Ecclesiastical court jurisdiction conflicts: Gallicanism in the Parlements, appels comme d'abus (French).
