# PART IV: IDEOLOGICAL POWER (pillar \mathsf{I}; source function K). "Who shapes the beliefs?"

## IV.1 Role
Owns: legitimation, ideology, hegemony, public sphere, media systems, academia/education, religious institutions, professional expertise (including healthcare and food-standard expertise in their knowledge role only), scientific and medical authority, propaganda, censorship, civil religion. Source Section 4.3 and 7.3 caution: hospital is not a propaganda organ; professor is not an agent of command; healthcare enters only as knowledge/standard-setting. **Disciplined statement for the paper:** healthcare appears in Part IV via professional authority (Starr 1982; Freidson; Larson; Abbott), clinical-evidence governance (Bekelman; Lexchin; Carpenter 2010), and via the conflict-of-interest literature; its service-delivery side belongs in Part VI (and benefits in source Prop 4/H6).
Notation: the pillar label is \mathsf{I}; the integration statistic is \mathcal{I}_t; the state-vector element is I_t^{cap}.

## IV.2 Necessity derivation (Ax4 coordination => I)
- **Axiom:** Ax4: cooperation requires shared beliefs about rules, roles and expectations that are *common knowledge* (Lewis 1969; Schelling 1960; Aumann 1976; Chwe 2001). Someone must produce, transmit and validate such beliefs (who counts as a member, an authority, a wrongdoer). Hart's rule of recognition is the legal version; Weber's legitimacy the political one; Gramsci's hegemony and Bourdieu's symbolic power the sociological ones.
- **Minimal informal form:** myth and ritual (n/um healing dance, initiation among the Nuer, Hadza epeme: Woodburn), moral talk and gossip circulating norms (boehm1999; boehm2012; wiessner2005), kinship idiom, naming and ridicule, elders as repositories of lore. Chwe 2001: rituals create common knowledge.
- **Strongest counterexample attempt:** pure coordination by incentives/force with no shared belief (Wintrobe's "tinpot" dictatorship; Tullock's autocracy; Weber's recognition that obedience can be from fear). Reply: even coercion-only regimes depend on common knowledge of the sanction rule and of each other's compliance (Kuran 1991 preference falsification; Svolik; Gerschewski's three pillars list legitimation as separate from repression but all need information); propaganda in modern autocracies targeted at signaling strength (Guriev-Treisman). Second attempt: markets coordinate by prices without shared ideology (Hayek); reply: prices presuppose shared property conventions (recognized, taught, defended). **Falsifier:** a persistent collective whose members do not share any common-knowledge conventions about rules or authority.
- **Dependency note:** I needs W surplus to support specialists (priests, scribes, teachers); I provides recognition to P, J, C; F funds I (patronage, endowments, advertising).

## IV.3 Key concepts
1. **Hegemony** (gramsci1971): consent organized through civil society; **Ideological State Apparatuses** (althusser1971); **symbolic power** and fields (bourdieu1991; bourdieu1989; bourdieu1977).
2. **Mann's IEMP** (mann1986; mann1993): ideological power from meaning, norms and aesthetic/ritual practices, immanent and transcendent forms; closest existing framework. Mann also: world religions as ideological networks (e.g., Christendom, Islam) giving transnational extensive reach.
3. **Three dimensions of power** (lukes2005), **non-decisions** (bachrach1962).
4. **Propaganda model** (herman1988): five filters (ownership, advertising, sourcing, flak, anti-communism). Contested: demand-side evidence (gentzkow2006), pluralism of outlets (hallin2004). Required: pair with critics and measurement.
5. **Media systems** (hallin2004: liberal, democratic corporatist, polarized pluralist), **media capture** (besley2006), **ownership** (djankov2003), **persuasion effects** (dellavigna2007; enikolopov2011), **authoritarian censorship** (king2013; stockmann2013), **informational autocrats / spin dictators** (guriev2019; guriev2022).
6. **Public sphere** (habermas1989; fraser1990): rational-critical debate and its refeudalization; counterpublics.
7. **Professions and expert authority** (larson1977; abbott1988; freidson1970; starr1982): cultural authority converts into economic and political authority; **conflicts of interest** (bekelman2003; lexchin2003); **regulatory reputation** (carpenter2010); **academic capitalism** (slaughter2004); **organized doubt** (oreskes2010) vs **social account of trust** (oreskes2019).
8. **Religious institutions as ideological power:** ulama and madrasa networks (zaman2002; makdisi1981; berkey1992; hefner2007; eickelman1992; eickelman1996), Gellner's thesis (gellner1981) that Islam's scripturalism and ulama shape high culture; Ottoman Seyhulislam; Iranian clerical state (arjomand1988; menashri1992); Saudi religious establishment (commins2006; alrasheed2002); Catholic Church: Counter-Reformation, Jesuit schools, Index (hsia1998; omalley1993; fragnito2001), Gallicanism (vankley1996), Louis XIV's image (burke1992), divine right (bossuet1990; kantorowicz1957), the Church as state-building template (grzymala2023; grzymala2015); religion-state relations generally (stepan2000; casanova1994; philpott2007; fox2008; kuru2009; kuru2019 vs rubin2017; becker2009).
9. **Print and communication technology** (eisenstein1979; anderson1983; gellner1983).
10. **Soviet agitprop and Stalinist public culture** (kenez1985; brooks2000), **commissariat of enlightenment** (Fitzpatrick 1970: gap).

## IV.4 Six-regime comparison table
| Regime | Who shapes belief | Illustration 1 | Illustration 2 | Accountability / pluralism | Key refs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Market-capitalist | Media firms, universities (public and private), think tanks, pharma/industry-funded research, advertising, professions | Corporate sponsorship and research outcome (systematic reviews of conflicts of interest); regulator reputation (FDA) | Media ownership concentration vs consumer-driven slant; Fox News effect on voting | competition among outlets, peer review, COI disclosure, replication, litigation | bekelman2003, lexchin2003, carpenter2010, slaughter2004, herman1988, gentzkow2006, dellavigna2007, djankov2003, starr1982, oreskes2010 |
| 2 Communist party-state | Party propaganda department, state media, party schools, ideological education (Marxism-Leninism), writers' unions | Soviet agitprop and Stalinist public culture: press as ritual of gratitude | PRC: censorship of collective expression; commercialized but party-guided media; informational autocracy | party discipline; elite factions; samizdat/self-publication; market pressure | kenez1985, brooks2000, king2013, stockmann2013, guriev2019, guriev2022 |
| 3 Democratic / market socialism, social democracy | Public service broadcasters; public universities; unions and popular education movements (folk high schools); churches | Democratic corporatist media model (Northern Europe): public subsidy, press-party parallelism history | Educational expansion by social-democratic governments; ideas and party agency | independence of public media from party; academic freedom statutes | hallin2004, berman2006, rothstein1996, esping1990 |
| 4 Islamic states | Ulama (jurists, teachers), madrasa/waqf-financed education, mosque sermons, state religious affairs bodies (Diyanet, Saudi Ministry of Islamic Affairs, Iran's seminary system and Supreme Council of the Cultural Revolution) | Ulama as independent custodians of knowledge vs rulers' patronage: medieval madrasa/college | Iran and Saudi Arabia: clerical state education and media; Ottoman Seyhulislam | juristic pluralism (four Sunni schools; Shia marja'iyya independent financing via khums), print/satellite fragmentation of authority (Eickelman-Piscatori) | zaman2002, makdisi1981, berkey1992, hefner2007, eickelman1992, eickelman1996, gellner1981, menashri1992, arjomand1988, commins2006, imber1997, kuru2019, rubin2017 |
| 5 Historical Catholic monarchies | Church hierarchy, universities (Salamanca, Paris/Sorbonne), religious orders (Jesuits), court culture, Inquisition and Index, printing licensing | Counter-Reformation: Jesuit education and Index of Prohibited Books; Inquisition as censor | Bourbon image-making (Louis XIV) and Gallican/Jansenist conflicts over religious authority | Gallican liberties; Jansenist counter-publics; competing orders; Protestant rivals | hsia1998, omalley1993, fragnito2001, burke1992, vankley1996, bossuet1990, kantorowicz1957, eisenstein1979, kamen1997 |
| 6 Secular democracies | Free press, universities, scientific academies, civil society; religion separated or established-but-plural | Public sphere and its structural transformation; counterpublics | Church-state arrangements: passive vs assertive secularism; religion's public role | constitutional speech protections; competition; independent professions | habermas1989, fraser1990, kuru2009, stepan2000, casanova1994, oreskes2019, hallin2004 |

## IV.5 Candidate hypotheses
- **HI1 (state ownership of media).** State-owned media share is associated with worse press freedom indicators and weaker accountability outcomes (djankov2003). *Falsifier:* no association after controls; public-service broadcasting in democratic corporatist systems (hallin2004) as outlier set.
- **HI2 (demand vs supply of slant).** Variation in outlet slant tracks audience demand more than owner preferences (gentzkow2006), contra a strong propaganda-model prediction. *Falsifier:* owner changes alter content independent of audience composition.
- **HI3 (sponsorship).** Industry-sponsored studies report favorable conclusions more often (bekelman2003; lexchin2003); test whether disclosure rules (Q^I) reduce the gap (source H4). *Falsifier:* no reduction.
- **HI4 (informational autocracy).** Regimes with greater economic-performance information and media control shift from fear to spin (Guriev-Treisman). *Falsifier:* no shift.
- **HI5 (authority fragmentation by mass communication).** Mass education and new media multiply claimants to religious authority (Eickelman-Piscatori), lowering ulama monopoly, in Islamic states; parallel for print and the Reformation in Catholic Europe (Eisenstein; Pettegree: gap).
- **HI6 (church as state-building template).** European states copied church organization and religious-moral authority; where the Church was strong/autonomous, rulers' legitimating choices differ (Grzymala-Busse). *Falsifier:* no association.
- **HI7 (pairwise coupling).** C_{IP} (religious/party-ideological organs appoint or vet political candidates) is high in Iran-style and party-state systems, low in separation-of-church-state democracies. *Falsifier:* no difference.
- **HI8 (expertise as check).** Independent science funding diversity predicts higher rates of documented error correction (source H4). *Falsifier:* none.

## IV.6 Measurement
V-Dem media and academic freedom indicators; Freedom House / RSF press freedom (verify), Fox's religion-state coding (fox2008), media ownership data (djankov2003), COI disclosure rates; content analysis and audit designs (king2013). Always test measurement invariance.

## IV.7 Gaps requiring web research
1. Pettegree 2005 (Reformation and the Culture of Persuasion) and 2010 (Book in the Renaissance): not verified here.
2. Healthcare authority: add outcomes evidence for public vs private systems only in Part VI; Part IV needs sociology of professions beyond Starr, e.g., Light, Hafferty; Cochrane review of sponsorship (Lundh et al. 2017; DOI 10.1002/14651858.MR000033.pub3; unverified here).
3. Iranian seminary autonomy and khums; Al-Azhar's relationship with the Egyptian state; Diyanet.
4. Chinese ideological apparatus (propaganda department): Brady 2008 Marketing Dictatorship; Shambaugh 2007 China Quarterly 'China's Propaganda System'.
5. Evidence reviews contesting propaganda-model predictions (Boyd-Barrett, Klaehn collections; Mullen).
6. Gallup / Edelman trust data for institutional trust in the six regime types (use carefully: source Section 10.2).

# PART V: COERCIVE POWER (pillar \mathsf{C}; source function E). "Who enforces the rules?"

## V.1 Role
Owns: monopoly of legitimate force, military, police, intelligence, prisons, civil-military relations, praetorianism, coup-proofing, repression, secret police, military-fiscal bargaining, mercenaries/janissary-type military slavery, paramilitaries. Source: coercion measured by discretionary and asymmetric deployment, not spending (Section 4.4). Q^C: civilian control, judicial authorization, complaints mechanisms, legal accountability, ability of militaries/police to refuse unlawful orders. Shared objects: appointment channel (II), enforcement of judgments (III), fiscal basis of coercion (I).

## V.2 Necessity derivation (Ax5 defection => C)
- **Axiom:** Ax5: some members sometimes break rules, and rules unenforced erode (Hobbes ch. 17: covenants without the sword are but words; Axelrod: cooperation requires retaliation capacity; Fehr-Gachter: costly punishment sustains cooperation).
- **Minimal informal form:** gossip, ridicule, criticism, avoidance, ostracism, exile and in extreme cases execution of deviants or would-be dominants (boehm1999; boehm2012; wiessner2005); Ju/'hoansi teasing and avoidance; Nuer feud and balanced opposition with compensation (evanspritchard1940); Clastres: chief who commands is abandoned or killed (clastres1987); Woodburn: institutions of leveling (woodburn1982). Sanction capacity is distributed (H^C near 1/n) and rarely specialized.
- **Strongest counterexample attempt:** self-enforcing equilibria with no punishment institution: Maghribi traders' reputation coalition (greif1993); repeated-game cooperation (axelrod1984); Ostrom commons (ostrom1990). Reply: multilateral reputation sanctions are sanctions (collective refusal to deal); Ostrom's own design principles include monitoring and graduated sanctions (ostromdesign2010; cox2010 for empirical review); thus a distributed C. A different counterexample, a population that never defects, contradicts Ax5. **Falsifier:** a persistent collective with observed rule-breaking and neither material nor social sanction of any kind.
- **Dependency note:** C needs F (provisioning of the sanctioners; Tilly), I (the sanction must be recognized as legitimate), J (procedures that distinguish wrongdoing).

## V.3 Key concepts
1. **Monopoly of legitimate violence** (weber1946; weber1978) and **state formation through war** (tilly1990; tilly1985): coercion-intensive vs capital-intensive paths; "protection racket" analogy (a heuristic, not equivalence).
2. **Despotic vs infrastructural power** (mann1984; mann1986): enforcement capacity vs arbitrary deployment (source Section 2.3).
3. **Military revolution and fiscal-military state** (parker1988; parker1972; thompson1976; rowlands2002; lynn1997; parrott2012; karaman2013; agoston2005).
4. **Coup-proofing** (quinlivan1999): exploitation of family/ethnic ties, parallel forces, counterbalancing, recruiting; trade-off military effectiveness. **Coercive institutions and secret police** (greitens2016): threat environment (mass vs elite) determines design (fragmented vs unified). **Repression** (davenport2007; poe1994; hill2014; wintrobe1998). **Protection pacts** (slater2010). **Robustness of coercive apparatus** (bellin2004; bellin2012; albrecht2016; chenoweth2011). **Coup data** (powell2011).
5. **Civil-military relations:** objective vs subjective control (huntington1957), professional soldier (janowitz1960), agency (feaver1999; feaver2003), praetorianism (huntington1968; perlmutter1969; finer1962), democratic control (kohn1997), intelligence oversight (born2007), torture in democracies (rejali2007), military-industrial complex (eisenhower1961).
6. **Police legitimacy** (tyler1990; jackson2012) and Nordic penal moderation (pratt2008a; pratt2008b).
7. **Party-army relations in communist states** (colton1979; shambaugh2002), **secret police** (leggett1981; knight1996; conquest1990; gieseke2014; bruce2010; koehler1999), **PRC police** (scoggins2016).
8. **Slave soldiers** (crone1980; pipes1981; irwin1986; kunt1983; kennedy2001): mamluks and janissaries as military instruments of rulers who could not trust local elites; the line to the kul/devsirme system; internal diversity (Abbasid ghilman, Mamluk sultanate, Ottoman janissary corps that became a political actor).
9. **Iran:** IRGC as ideological guardian with political-economic roles (wehrey2009; katzman1993; alfoneh2013; arjomand1988). **Saudi:** dual army/National Guard structure and religious legitimation (cordesman2003; gause1994; hegghammer2007).
10. **Catholic monarchies:** army of Flanders and Spanish Road (parker1972), venal officer corps (rowlands2002; lynn1997), Swiss mercenaries and papal forces (alvarez2011), military enterprise (parrott2012), Church-militant orders (Teutonic/Calatrava: gap), Inquisition's familiars as quasi-police (kamen1997).

## V.4 Six-regime comparison table
| Regime | Who enforces | Illustration 1 | Illustration 2 | Accountability / checks | Key refs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Market-capitalist | Police and military under elected civilians; private security and contractors; intelligence agencies | US civil-military relations and Eisenhower's 1961 warning on the military-industrial complex | Democratic torture and intelligence oversight problems | civilian control, courts, legislative committees, press | huntington1957, feaver2003, eisenhower1961, rejali2007, born2007, kohn1997, parrott2012 |
| 2 Communist party-state | Party-controlled security organs (Cheka-OGPU-NKVD-KGB; Stasi), party commissars in army, militia | Cheka to KGB: organs of party control; Stasi informant network | Party-army relations in USSR and PRC; PRC police and stability maintenance | party discipline; purge risk; limited external oversight | leggett1981, knight1996, conquest1990, gieseke2014, bruce2010, koehler1999, colton1979, shambaugh2002, scoggins2016, greitens2016 |
| 3 Democratic / market socialism, social democracy | Police and armed forces under parliamentary control; Nordic conscription tradition; ombudsmen | Nordic police legitimacy and penal moderation | Post-conscription civil-military relations in Sweden (Armed Forces & Society articles found; cite after verifying authors) | procedural justice, ombudsman, parliamentary scrutiny | tyler1990, jackson2012, pratt2008a, pratt2008b, janowitz1960, feaver1999 |
| 4 Islamic states | Caliphal and sultanic armies, slave-soldier corps, tribal levies, religious-ideological forces | Mamluks and Ottoman janissaries: slave soldiers who became political actors | Iran's IRGC and Basij; Saudi National Guard-Army duality as coup-proofing | variable: ulama as moral check historically; coup risk; jurisdictional separation of forces | crone1980, pipes1981, irwin1986, kunt1983, kennedy2001, agoston2005, wehrey2009, alfoneh2013, katzman1993, cordesman2003, gause1994, hegghammer2007, quinlivan1999, bellin2004 |
| 5 Historical Catholic monarchies | Royal armies (venal and noble officers), mercenary companies, Swiss troops, papal troops and Swiss Guard, military orders, Inquisition familiars | Habsburg Spain: Army of Flanders, mutinies, military finance | Bourbon France: Louis XIV army, venal commissions, noble service; Papal armed forces | estates and royal finance limit; mutinies; condottieri contracts; Church sanctions (interdict, excommunication) as ideological-coercive hybrid | parker1972, parker1988, thompson1976, rowlands2002, lynn1997, parrott2012, alvarez2011, kamen1997, tilly1990 |
| 6 Secular democracies | Civilian-controlled professional forces; police with judicial authorization; independent inspectorates | Huntingtonian and Feaver models across democracies | Coup rarity and civilian control practices; legitimacy-based policing | judicial warrants, parliamentary oversight, civil society | huntington1957, feaver2003, kohn1997, born2007, powell2011, tyler1990 |

## V.5 Candidate hypotheses
- **HC1 (threat environment).** Regimes facing greater elite threat build fragmented, loyalty-based coercive institutions; those facing mass threat build intrusive unified secret police (Greitens). *Falsifier:* design unrelated to threat environment.
- **HC2 (coup-proofing trade-off).** Coup-proofing reduces coup success but also military effectiveness (Quinlivan; Pilster-Boehmelt: gap). *Falsifier:* no effect on effectiveness.
- **HC3 (security-force defection).** Cohesion/ethnic composition and professionalism predict defection vs repression during unrest (Bellin; Albrecht-Ohl; Chenoweth-Stephan). *Falsifier:* no association.
- **HC4 (fiscal basis).** The mode of financing of coercion (taxation vs rents vs foreign aid) shapes accountability (Tilly; Karaman-Pamuk; Ross). *Falsifier:* no relationship.
- **HC5 (slave-soldier logic).** Rulers rely on outsider/dependent soldiers where local elites are untrustworthy; outsider corps eventually become political actors (Crone; Pipes). *Falsifier:* outsider corps remain subordinate.
- **HC6 (democracy and repression).** Democracy and judicial independence reduce state repression (Poe-Tate; Hill-Jones). *Falsifier:* no effect after conflict controls; torture in democracies (Rejali) as boundary case.
- **HC7 (fusion C-F).** Military business activity (IRGC, PLA historically, Egyptian military) raises C_{FC} and U^F; rival: public-goods provision. *Falsifier:* no association with contestability.
- **HC8 (interrupt, source Prop 2/Section 11.4).** Independent complaints mechanisms and judicial authorization lower politically selective sanctions; reporting effects must be separated from behavior changes (source).

## V.6 Measurement
Military expenditure share is NOT the measure (source). Use: share of security chiefs appointed/removable by a single actor; existence/independence of oversight; politically selective arrests; V-Dem political-killings and civil liberties indicators; coup datasets (powell2011); Political Terror Scale and CIRI (verify, not checked); IRGC/army business share (needs primary data).

## V.7 Gaps requiring web research
1. Verified scholarship on the Swiss Guard and papal armed forces before 1796 (Alvarez covers from 1796); condottieri literature (Mallett 1974 Mercenaries and Their Masters).
2. PRC People's Armed Police and domestic security budget (Wang Yuhua 2014; Xie; unverified).
3. Mamluk/janissary primary-scholarship: Ayalon's collected studies; Goodwin; Murphey.
4. Nordic police and military: authors of "Civil-Military Control over the Swedish Military Profession" (Armed Forces & Society 39(1), 2013) must be found.
5. Intelligence oversight in democracies: Gill and Phythian; Lowenthal; the Church Committee (1975-76) primary.
6. Quantitative: Pilster-Boehmelt on coup-proofing; Sudduth.

# PART VI: PRODUCTIVE POWER (pillar \mathsf{W}; source function W). "Who produces the wealth that sustains everything?"

## VI.1 Role
Owns: workers, taxpayers, farmers, businesses, consumers, households; labor markets, unions, serfdom/tithes/corvee/kolkhoz, tax compliance and the fiscal contract, welfare regimes, exit/voice/loyalty, everyday resistance, rentier-state labor (migrant labor), dependency. Source Section 4.5 and 7.5: W is active, not passive; dependency is not powerlessness; taxation is not automatically extraction; slavery language is disciplined (Section 3). Q^W: bargaining coverage, mobility (labor and benefit portability), legal access, income protection, voice.
Special notation: the series' state-vector element W_t and pillar label \mathsf{W}; avoid the symbol for wage.

## VI.2 Necessity derivation (Ax6 maintenance => W)
- **Axiom:** Ax6: the group and its institutions (including the other five functions) consume resources that someone must produce. A governance system that persists for more than one cycle must be supplied by production (foraging, farming, craft, exchange, tribute from outside producers, or machines).
- **Minimal informal form:** household and band labor in foraging (lee1979: roughly 15-20 hours a week of food-getting among the Dobe Ju/'hoansi according to Lee; verify the figure before quoting), seasonal farming and herding (evanspritchard1940), chiefs who work harder (clastres1987), Scott's swidden and escape crops (scott2009). Here W is the *entire* membership: H^W ~ 1/n, Q^W high (exit by fission).
- **Strongest counterexample attempt:** (a) raiding/predation/tribute: production by others; reply: the producers exist outside the group; W is relocated (Ottoman reaya, helots, tributary peasants) and the system's dependency on W is merely hidden; (b) rentier systems with imported labor (Gulf kafala: hanieh2011, kapiszewski2001); (c) automation or stock consumption. Reply: automation relocates W to capital/machines owned by someone (H^W concentrates); stock-consuming systems are non-persistent by definition of persistence. **Falsifier:** a persistent governance system supported by no production and no stock replenishment.
- **Dependency note:** W <-> F (production creates stock, allocation sustains producers), W -> C and I (surplus supports specialists), I and C -> W (norms and compulsion for work and tax: Levi's quasi-voluntary compliance).

## VI.3 Key concepts
1. **Labor and surplus** (marx1976), **double movement and fictitious commodities** (polanyi1944): embedded vs disembedded markets; social protection as response.
2. **Exit, voice, loyalty** (hirschman1970): source's language for W; **everyday resistance** and **moral economy** (scott1985; scott1976); **legibility** (scott1998).
3. **Fiscal sociology** (schumpeter1954; campbell1993; martin2009), **revenue and rule** (levi1988): ruler's revenue maximization constrained by bargaining power, transaction costs, discount rates; quasi-voluntary compliance; **taxation and representation** (bates1985; ross2004; moore2004; timmons2005; besley2013; besley2011; scheve2016).
4. **Welfare regimes** (esping1990; korpi1983; korpi1998; huber2001; rothstein1996): decommodification; universal vs targeted; power resources.
5. **Labor-market power** (manning2003; azar2022), **unions** (freeman1984; western2011; farber2021), **distribution** (piketty2018; piketty2014).
6. **Unfree labor and bondage forms:** serfdom and the land-labor ratio (domar1970; blum1961; brenner1976), tithes (constable1964), corvee and colonial forced labor (dell2010: the mita), kolkhoz and procurement (fitzpatrick1994; viola1996; davies2004), Gulag (applebaum2003; gregory2003), hukou (cheng1994). **Terminology rule:** do not call wage labor or taxation slavery; reserve "bondage" for legally restricted mobility and compulsion.
7. **State socialist workplaces:** danwei dependence (walder1986), Stakhanovism (siegelbaum1988), labor discipline and worker evasion (filtzer1986), Magnitogorsk (kotkin1995), Chinese labor protest (lee2007; friedman2010).
8. **Rentier state and labor:** rentier theory (mahdavy1970; beblawiluciani1987; ross2001; chaudhry1997; hertog2010), Gulf migrant labor (hanieh2011; kapiszewski2001), Iranian workers and poor (bayat1987; bayat1997), Ottoman workers and guilds (quataert1995; baer1970), Islamic taxes (zakat, kharaj, jizya; ibn Khaldun on taxation: khaldun1958; singer2008).
9. **Catholic monarchies:** French peasants and royal fiscal bargains (root1987; hoffman1996; kwass2000; velde1992), Castilian peasants (vassberg1984), Spanish colonial mita (dell2010), tithes and Church property (constable1964; ekelund1996), taille/gabelle exemptions as privilege (kwass2000).

## VI.4 Six-regime comparison table
| Regime | Who produces/pays; terms | Illustration 1 | Illustration 2 | Voice / exit / checks | Key refs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Market-capitalist | Wage earners, small business, consumers, taxpayers; employer concentration; unions weak or strong | Employer concentration (monopsony) depresses wages in US local labor markets; union decline and wage inequality | Distribution of national income across top and bottom; fiscal bargain and tax compliance | collective bargaining, labor mobility, antitrust, vote | azar2022, manning2003, freeman1984, western2011, farber2021, piketty2018, levi1988, scheve2016 |
| 2 Communist party-state | State-employed workers, collective farmers, peasants under procurement; party-controlled unions | Collectivization and kolkhoz: peasant resistance and survival; procurement famine 1931-33 | Hukou system and danwei dependence; contemporary Chinese labor protests | everyday resistance (scott1985), exit via migration, informal bargaining; state-sanctioned unions | fitzpatrick1994, viola1996, davies2004, filtzer1986, siegelbaum1988, kotkin1995, cheng1994, walder1986, lee2007, friedman2010, applebaum2003 |
| 3 Democratic / market socialism, social democracy | High-density unions, universal welfare, high tax compliance; encompassing bargaining | Nordic welfare regimes: decommodification and union density | Power resources and redistribution paradox | strong voice; risk: insiders vs outsiders (check) | esping1990, korpi1983, korpi1998, huber2001, rothstein1996, pontusson1992 |
| 4 Islamic states | Peasants (reaya), artisans in guilds, merchants, migrant labor (Gulf), citizens receiving rents | Ottoman guilds and workers; Islamic taxation (kharaj, jizya, zakat) and Ibn Khaldun on tax rates | Gulf kafala system and nationals vs expatriates; Iranian workers, shoras (1979) and poor people's movements | guilds and qadi courts as venues; rent distribution as substitute for tax bargaining (Ross); migrant workers have little voice | baer1970, quataert1995, khaldun1958, singer2008, hanieh2011, kapiszewski2001, bayat1987, bayat1997, ross2001, beblawiluciani1987 |
| 5 Historical Catholic monarchies | Peasants owing seigneurial dues, tithe, taille; privileged exempt estates; colonial Indigenous labor | French countryside: peasant communities and royal fiscal deals; privilege as fiscal sorting | Spanish colonial mita and encomienda; Castilian peasants | village communities, courts (kagan1981 for Castile), Cortes; revolts | root1987, hoffman1996, kwass2000, vassberg1984, dell2010, constable1964, domar1970, brenner1976 |
| 6 Secular democracies | As in 1 and 3 plus universal suffrage, tax states with citizenship | Taxation and representation; fiscal contract | Welfare and labor market regulation | elections, fiscal compliance rooted in perceived fairness | timmons2005, ross2004, besley2013, martin2009, moore2004, schumpeter1954, campbell1993 |

## VI.5 Candidate hypotheses
- **HW1 (H5 specialised).** Higher bargaining coverage and benefit portability weaken the translation of employer concentration into wage markdowns. *Falsifier:* markdown unaffected by coverage.
- **HW2 (fiscal contract).** Where taxes are a large share of revenue, governments are more responsive to taxpayers (Bates-Lien; Timmons; Ross finds mixed evidence). *Falsifier:* no relationship; Ross 2004 shows not always.
- **HW3 (rentier effect).** Non-tax revenue reduces taxpayer leverage (Ross 2001; Beblawi). *Falsifier:* Haber-Menaldo-type null.
- **HW4 (land-labor ratio).** Bondage forms arise where land is abundant relative to labor and the state/lords can enforce (Domar); contested by Brenner (class relations). Test through regional variation.
- **HW5 (everyday resistance).** Where overt voice is closed, covert non-compliance rates (foot-dragging, evasion) rise (Scott; Fitzpatrick; Filtzer). *Falsifier:* no relation to repression level.
- **HW6 (insiders/outsiders).** In encompassing welfare states, benefits to insiders can raise dependency of outsiders (check literature).
- **HW7 (persistent extraction).** Historical forced-labor institutions (mita) leave persistent lower consumption and development (Dell). *Falsifier:* no persistence.
- **HW8 (public goods rival, source H6).** Higher tax take with accountable provision correlates with lower worker vulnerability. *Falsifier:* vulnerability increases with provision.

## VI.6 Measurement
Union density and bargaining coverage (OECD/ILOSTAT: verify), employer concentration (HHI of local vacancies: Azar et al.), tax compliance (rates), social insurance replacement rates, migrant share of labor force, labor protest event datasets (China Labour Bulletin: unverified). Use incidence analysis before calling a flow extractive (source Section 4.5).

## VI.7 Gaps requiring web research
1. Soviet and Chinese worker agency: Perry 1993 (Shanghai on Strike: unverified), Chan-Pun.
2. Catholic Church tithe incidence in early modern Europe: Hoffman, Tithe studies; Spanish diezmo.
3. Islamic law of taxation: Cahen's EI2 entries; Dennett 1950.
4. Welfare-state insiders/outsiders (Rueda 2007; Emmenegger et al. 2012).
5. Platform/gig work and exit in capitalist democracies; remote monitoring.
6. Dependence measures: switching costs data (job-to-job flows).

# PART VII: THE FORMALIZED STATE: THE HIERARCHY OF THE BEAST SYSTEM (all pillars; source Sections 4-5, 9)

## VII.1 Role
Owns: the six-layer formal model (M = 6), Proposition A (structural necessity, proved as a theorem), Proposition B (capture, conditional), the non-implication A -/-> B, the dependency matrix, the integration statistic \mathcal{I}_t with 15 pairs, the strong-classification criterion, comparison with rival frameworks (Mann, Montesquieu, Poggi, Acemoglu-Robinson, Fukuyama, North-Wallis-Weingast, Walzer, Bourdieu), the theological reading (Section 3 of this file). Part VII cites the pillar Parts for pillar-specific objects and never redefines objects in notation.md (the source owns them).
The proof architecture and functionalism safeguards are in Section 4 above; the stateless hard cases in Section 5. Part VII must not duplicate them but must (a) formalize and (b) discharge the open problems flagged there.

## VII.2 Necessity derivation: the theorem to prove (Proposition A)
- **Axioms.** Ax1 scarcity, Ax2 plurality, Ax3 generality, Ax4 coordination, Ax5 defection, Ax6 maintenance (Section 4.2). Part VII refines them: each should be stated as a precise assumption on a model of collective life (e.g., a repeated game with heterogeneous payoffs, a finite resource stock, a rule system with open texture, a common-knowledge requirement for equilibrium selection, a nonzero defection probability, a non-zero consumption rate), and persistence defined (the collective survives T periods with positive probability).
- **Derivation of each function (lemma schema).** For each k: Axk + persistence => the corresponding problem must be solved by some regularity; define the function as the *equivalence class of mechanisms that solve that problem*; then no persistent G exists without a member of the class. Express as: for every G in the class of persistent collectives, the role map has full support on M.
- **Minimal informal forms:** Section 5 table (Nuer, Clastres, Boehm, Ju/'hoansi, Woodburn, Scott, Graeber-Wengrow).
- **Strongest counterexample attempts to confront (one per axiom; see Part sections):** original affluent society (F); evolved custom without legislature (P); code is law (J); incentive-only coordination (I); self-enforcing reputation (C); raiding/automation/stock consumption (W). For each state the reply and the falsifier.
- **Derived dependency structure D** (Section 4.3 item 3): propose the 6x6 Boolean matrix; to prove: strong connectivity under Ax1-6. A sketch: W -> F (output), F -> W (inputs), F -> C (provisioning), C -> J (enforcement of rulings), J -> P (precedent), P -> I (norm declarations; legislation as public symbolic act), I -> W (norms of work/compliance). That is a directed cycle W -> F -> C -> J -> P -> I -> W containing all six nodes, so strong connectivity follows from the existence of each edge. Each edge needs its own lemma with an axiom-based justification; some may be weaker than "necessary" (e.g., J -> P is necessary only if rules change by interpretation; flag this). **Honest warning:** the cycle is a proof strategy, not a proof.
- **Non-implication A -/-> B (theorem 2).** Provide two explicit models (G1 plural-checked: 6 functions, many holders, Q near 1, C_{mn} near 0 => U_t near 0, I_t near 0; G2 fused: one holder, Q near 0 => U_t = sum omega_m H_m (1 - Q_m) near 1 and I_t near 1). Both satisfy A. Only G2 satisfies B. Therefore A does not imply B. Add the stronger claim: B implies A (capture presupposes the functions exist). Check: B => A is trivial since B includes the six-function structure by definition of integration C_{mn}.
- **Independence from definitional triviality:** state explicitly what a falsifying observation for A would look like (a persistent collective with Ax1-6 but no implemented function); if all candidate counterexamples fail on empirical grounds, A is supported, not "true by definition".

## VII.3 The six-layer formal model (series versions of source objects)
- M = {F, P, J, I, C, W}, |M| = 6; 15 pairs; polity multiplex G_t with layers A^m_t; role map; s^m_{i,t}; H_{m,t}; Q_{m,t}; U_t; C_{mn,t}; \mathcal{I}_t = (2/(6*5)) sum_{m<n} C_{mn,t} = (1/15) sum C_{mn,t}; capture link; x_t = (F_t, P_t, J_t, I^{cap}_t, C_t, W_t); x_{t+1} = B_t x_t + D_t z_t + eps_t; coalition objective.
- **Properties worth proving (suggestions, not in source):** (i) 0 <= U_t <= 1 under the weights simplex since H in [1/n, 1] and Q in [0,1]; (ii) U_t = 0 iff every pillar has Q = 1 or ... (H > 0 always; so U_t = 0 iff Q_m = 1 for all m with omega_m > 0); (iii) \mathcal{I}_t in [0,1] with extreme values for disjoint/fused coalitions; (iv) monotonicity: if a coalition adds control of pillar n while holding m, C_{mn} weakly increases (requires definition of C as a function of coalition overlap, e.g., a normalized overlap of top-share holders: define C_{mn,t} = max over coalitions S of min(s^m(S), s^n(S)) or some preregistered variant; **the source leaves C undefined beyond description**, so Part VII should propose and test one or two formal definitions, e.g., overlap coefficient of top-k holders, or probability two random decisions in m and n are controlled by the same coalition); (v) H_{m,t} bounded below by 1/|V_t|: indexing by number of actors N means comparisons across polities of different size need normalization (H* = (H - 1/N)/(1 - 1/N)); the source does not normalize.
- **Dynamics:** B_t's spectral radius as persistence; positive off-diagonal entries (the derived dependencies D). Under the dependency cycle, B_t is irreducible: Perron-Frobenius implications (dominant eigenvalue real positive if B_t >= 0; eigenvector gives the "central" pillar). Danger: B_t non-negativity is an assumption (negative feedback exists: source Section 5.3).
- **Strong classification (source 9.5).** At least two functions with high U, demonstrated cross-pillar influence, persistent restriction of contestation, consequential outcome linked to the coalition. Formalize as a conjunction of four testable predicates with thresholds preregistered.
- **Recommended multilayer-network references** for the G_t formalism: kivela2014, boccaletti2014, dedomenico2013. **Concentration index references:** hirschman1964, rhoades1993. **Governance data:** kaufmann2010, coppedge2011, luhrmann2018, V-Dem v16 (source URL).

## VII.4 Comparison with rival frameworks (what each adds, what six pillars add)
| Framework | Components | Relation to F,P,J,I,C,W | Distinctive point |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mann IEMP (mann1986, mann1993) | ideological, economic, military, political networks | I=I, E~F(+W), M~C, P~P(+J) | no separate judicial pillar; no separate producer pillar; no capture index. Six pillars adds J and W and measures |
| Montesquieu / Madison (montesquieu1989; madison1788) | legislative, executive, judicial | P (legislative+executive), J | normative separation inside government only; ignores F, I, C, W |
| Weber class, status, party (weber1978) | economic order, social order, political order | F (class), I (status), P (party) | triad of power |
| Poggi (poggi1990; poggi1978) | state as differentiated political power: rules, administration, force, taxation, legitimation | P,J,C,F,I | state-centered; W weak |
| Acemoglu-Robinson (acemoglu2006, acemoglu2012, acemoglu2019) | de jure/de facto political power, extractive vs inclusive; Shackled Leviathan | B (extractive) vs non-B; focus on P and F | gives the contingent-capture theory (Prop B); A not stated |
| Fukuyama (fukuyama2011) | state, rule of law, accountability | P (state), J (law), accountability = Q | triad of conditions of political order |
| North-Wallis-Weingast (nww2009) | limited access vs open access orders; violence specialists, elites | C+F+P integrated in limited-access orders | closest to B as a typology: elites' rents buy peace |
| Walzer, Spheres of Justice (walzer1983) | distinct social goods with distinct distributive spheres; tyranny = conversion of dominance across spheres | each pillar a sphere; C_{mn} = conversion | normative backing for separation of pillars |
| Bourdieu capitals (bourdieu1989; bourdieu1991) | economic, cultural, social, symbolic capital; fields | F, I | convertibility among forms of capital = cross-pillar integration |
| Easton / Parsons (easton1965; parsons1951) | systems functions; AGIL | A~F/W, G~P, I~J/C, L~I | precedent for functional-prerequisite approach; see functionalism critiques |
| Block (block1977), Miliband (miliband1969) | instrumentalist vs structural Marxian state | B via conscious ties vs structure | Block's "ruling class does not rule": structural constraint without conspiracy: relevant to measurement of capture links |
| Pettit (pettit1997; pettit2012), Lukes | domination = capacity for arbitrary interference | supports Q definition | normative definition of domination used in source |
| Tilly (tilly1990) | capital and coercion | F,C | state formation |
| Ostrom (ostrom1990; ostromdesign2010) | design principles; polycentricity | all six, small-scale | checks in minimal form |

## VII.5 Integration across the six regime types (what \mathcal{I}_t patterns to expect; for illustration only, no numerical claim)
| Regime | Dominant integration pairs (hypothesis) | Illustration 1 | Illustration 2 | Rival explanation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Market-capitalist | C_{FP}, C_{FI}; C_{FJ} via repeat-player resources | Financial-sector lobbying and political access | Sponsorship of research and media | competing interest groups; pluralism (Dahl); money little in politics (Ansolabehere et al.) |
| 2 Communist party-state | C_{PF}, C_{PJ}, C_{PI}, C_{PC} via nomenklatura | CPSU appointment system | PRC party leadership of courts, SOEs, media | bureaucratic divisions (Lane-Ross); informal bargaining |
| 3 Social democracy | C_{PW}, C_{FW} via corporatist bargaining | Nordic tripartite bargaining | Wage-earner funds proposals | high Q^W (voice) offsets H; public goods (Prop 4) |
| 4 Islamic states | C_{PI}, C_{PJ} (religious-legal authority and rulers), C_{FC} in IRGC | Iran: Leader, Guardian Council, IRGC business | Saudi: royal decrees, ulama, oil rent allocation | historical juristic independence; diversity across Islamic polities |
| 5 Catholic monarchies | C_{PI}, C_{PJ}, C_{FP} (venality), C_{CF} (military enterprise) | Habsburg Spain: crown, Church, bankers | Bourbon France: venal offices, Gallican Church | Parlements, Cortes; Church-crown conflicts |
| 6 Secular democracies | all pairs moderate to low if Q high | constitutional separation and independent bodies | independent central banks and constitutional courts as delegated powers | delegation concentrates technocratic power (relocation of H) |

## VII.6 Candidate hypotheses (Part VII level)
- **HV1 (A):** no persistent collective in the ethnographic/historical record lacks any of the six functions in some form. *Falsifier:* a documented counterexample.
- **HV2 (A -/-> B):** across a panel of polities, role-map completeness is invariant while (U_t, \mathcal{I}_t) varies widely. *Falsifier:* U_t and \mathcal{I}_t collinear with role-map features (would suggest B is structurally forced).
- **HV3 (source H2):** high \mathcal{I}_t predicts persistence of preferential rules after disclosure.
- **HV4 (dependency symmetry):** estimated B_t has significant off-diagonal entries between all six pillars (irreducible).
- **HV5 (bridging):** dispersing control in one pillar without reducing cross-pillar overlap does not reduce persistence of preferential rules (Walzer's conversion).
- **HV6 (checks):** adding independent contestability Q in any single pillar reduces transmission across that pillar (source Prop 2); test with staggered reform (source event study).
- **HV7 (public benefit):** U_t and service quality are not monotonically related (source Prop 4/H6).

## VII.7 Gaps requiring web research
1. Verify Hart's Concept of Law page ranges for the three defects (ch. V) and 3rd edition publication details.
2. Aberle et al. 1950: confirm the list of prerequisites and Ethics pagination; check later critiques (e.g., Levy; Parsons).
3. Formal results on multilayer network concentration measures and cross-layer overlap (Battiston, Nicosia, Latora 2014 "Structural measures for multiplex networks" Phys Rev E 89:032804: unverified).
4. Formal social-choice treatments of power indices that could ground s^m (Shapley-Shubik, Banzhaf) for P pillar.
5. Interdisciplinary literature on "iron law of oligarchy" in non-party organizations.
6. Critiques of Graeber-Wengrow (e.g., reviews in Antiquity, Current Anthropology) and critiques of Clastres and Scott for hard-case section.
7. Revelation reception, e.g., Wink's trilogy, to see whether "Domination System" is commensurate with Proposition A.

# 10. CROSS-REFERENCES: SHARED CONCEPTS AND OWNERSHIP

**Ownership rule (from the brief):** the lower-numbered Part owns a shared object; objects defined in team/notation.md are owned by the source paper (cite `\citep{largo2026hbs}`, never redefine). Non-owner Parts cite the owner Part and use the object without re-deriving it.

| Concept / object | Appears in Parts | Owner | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| G_t, A^m_t, a^m_{ij,t}, role map, s^m, H, Q, U_t, C_{mn}, \mathcal{I}_t, capture link, x_t dynamics, coalition objective, strong HBS criterion, Props 1-4, H1-H6 | all | **source paper** | Part VII formalizes (does not redefine) |
| Proposition A (structural necessity) and its axioms Ax1-6, dependency matrix D, non-implication A -/-> B | all (derivation sub-blocks) | **Part VII** | Parts I-VI present only their pillar's derivation and cite VII for the theorem |
| Minimal-form lemma, hard cases | all | **Part VII** (Section 5 here) | each pillar paper reports its own row |
| Hart's rules of change/adjudication/recognition | II, III, IV, VII | **Part II** for change; III for adjudication; IV for recognition; VII for the triad | cite once each |
| Appointment channel (who appoints whom) | II, III, V, VII | **Part II** | III and V use it for judges and security chiefs |
| Agenda control / non-decision / three faces of power | II, IV | **Part II** | IV applies it to preferences |
| Capture, regulatory capture, rent seeking, soft budget constraint | I, II, III, VII | **Part I** | VII uses it as the mechanism for B |
| State capture (Hellman et al.) | I, II, VII | **Part I** | |
| Rentier state | I, V, VI | **Part I** (allocation side); VI uses the labor/tax side | |
| Fiscal-military state, war and state-making | I, II, V | **Part I** (financing side); V (coercion side) | Tilly cited in both; V owns Tilly's coercion thesis |
| Taxation / fiscal contract | I, VI | **Part I** (as revenue allocation); **VI** for compliance, fairness, bargaining | split explicitly in each paper |
| Repeat players, access to courts | I, III, VI | **Part I** introduces resource advantage; **III** owns the legal-mobilization object | |
| Independent courts / judicial independence / rule of law vs rule by law | III, V, VII | **Part III** | |

| Hegemony, legitimacy, ISAs, public sphere, symbolic power | II, IV, VII | **Part II** for legitimacy types (Weber); **IV** for hegemony, ISAs, public sphere | |

| Welfare regimes | I, VI | **Part VI** | |
| Unions and collective bargaining | I, III, VI | **Part VI** | |
| Bureaucratic divisions in party-states (Lane-Ross) | II, VII | **Part II** | |
| Revelation 13, 17-18 and interpretive schools | all, VII | **Part VII** (Section 3 here) | all papers use same short typological note |
| Michels's iron law and critics | II, VII | **Part II** | conditional axiom for B |
| Madison/Montesquieu separation of powers | II, III, VII | **Part II** | |
| Measurement (V-Dem, WGI) | all | **Part VII** | |

## Which sources recur across Parts (heavy reuse; cite from the first-owner Part's framing)
- Weber 1978 (II, III, V, VII), Mann 1984/1986 (II, IV, V, VII), Tilly 1990 (I, II, V), Hirschman 1970 (VI first-owner), Scott 1998 and 1985 (IV, VI), Kuran (I, VI), Ross (I, II, VI), Hallaq (II, III), Hirschl (III), Gilens-Page (I, II), Kornai (I, II, VI).

## Cross-pillar pairs (15) and which Parts treat the pair (assign each pair to the lower-numbered pillar as the primary treatment; the other cites)
FP: I; FJ: I; FI: I; FC: I; FW: I; PJ: II; PI: II; PC: II; PW: II; JI: III; JC: III; JW: III; IC: IV; IW: IV; CW: V. Part VII synthesizes all 15 and owns \mathcal{I}_t.

## Source-to-series reconciliation notes
1. Source L maps to P + J. Source Prop 3 (control paths) is split: wealth-to-rules is F->P; appointment-to-allocation is P->F; wealth/appointment to adjudication is F->J, P->J.
2. Source H1-H6 are generic; each pillar Part should state a pillar-specific refinement (see candidate hypotheses lists) and cite the H as parent.
3. Source treats healthcare and food within K; Part IV treats only knowledge/standard-setting aspects (see Part IV.1).
4. Source M = 5 in \mathcal{I}_t; series M = 6, 15 pairs.
5. Source calls the apex of the diagram R; the series states that the apex is regime-dependent (Prop 3) and avoids ordering the pillars.
6. All Parts: use `largo2026hbs` as the only BibTeX key for the source; do not cite the source's own references as if they were new discoveries.

## Disagreements and tensions in the literature the series must expose (even-handedness)
- Elite theory (Michels, Mosca, Mills, Winters) vs pluralism (Dahl, Baumgartner et al., Ansolabehere et al.) vs structural views (Lindblom, Block).
- Propaganda model (Herman-Chomsky) vs demand-driven bias (Gentzkow-Shapiro) vs informational autocrat model (Guriev-Treisman).
- Ulama-state alliance as cause of Middle East underdevelopment (Kuran 2011; Kuru 2019) vs bargaining and state-structure accounts (Rubin 2017; critics of the "Long Divergence" such as Pamuk; check) vs Hallaq's claim that classical Islamic governance was a moral-legal order without a modern state.
- Spanish Inquisition: black legend vs revisionism (Kamen) vs critiques of revisionism (verify).
- Absolutism: command model vs brokered model (Beik; Collins).
- Totalitarian model vs revisionist Soviet history (Fainsod vs Fitzpatrick/Filtzer/Lane-Ross).
- Gilens-Page vs Branham et al. and Elkjaer-Klitgaard.
- Hunter-gatherer baselines: Rousseauian egalitarian vs Hobbesian violent images; Boehm, Lee, Wiessner vs critics; Graeber-Wengrow reception.

| Part II political (P) | 54 | 54 | 220 |
| Part III judicial (J) | 55 | 55 | 71 |
| Part IV ideological (I) | 65 | 64 | 89 |
| Part V coercive (C) | 62 | 62 | 83 |

| Part VII frameworks and formal (V) | 47 | 45 | 90 |
| Theology (T) | 25 | 25 | 25 |
| Hard cases (H) | 25 | 25 | 26 |

**What VERIFIED means here (again):** DOI entries: Crossref returned a record whose title, first-author surname and year (within one year for articles) matched my entry. Catalog entries: Open Library lists a work with that title and author. URL entries: HTTP success. I did not read the works; pagination, edition details and publisher strings for books are from memory and may be off. Four entries (omalley1993, schumpeter1954, rhoades1993, block1977) have NO-ID status: neither Crossref nor Open Library gave a match I trusted, so cite them only after a manual check.

**Corrections made by verification (worth knowing):** many DOIs I first supplied from memory were wrong (82 of 200 supplied DOIs failed the automatic title/author/year test; 3 of those were false alarms that I restored by hand; the rest were replaced by Crossref-matched DOIs or dropped; mostly Cambridge book DOIs). The final DOIs come from Crossref searches that matched title and author, not from memory. Workers must not add DOIs from memory.

**Known imperfections:** (1) some Cambridge/Princeton book DOIs resolve to the book-level record, others to chapter-level DOIs: check; (2) years for articles follow publication-issue year where I knew it, but Crossref may show online-first dates (flagged in NOTE); (3) the Aune WBC volumes are catalog-verified (Open Library) with no DOI; (4) Wehrey et al. RAND URL returned HTTP 403 to my script (probably bot protection) so it is catalog-verified only; (5) the bibliography does not contain the Swedish Armed Forces & Society papers I found by web search because I could not read author names.


# APPENDIX A. WEB SEARCH LOG (items found by search, not from memory)
- Alvarez 2011, The Pope's Soldiers (Univ. Press of Kansas; covers Papal forces from 1796; first access to Swiss Guard archive): found via web search, then catalog-verified.
- Nordic civil-military relations: search returned Armed Forces & Society articles ("Anchoring Sweden's Post-conscript Military", 2018; "Civil-Military Control over the Swedish Military Profession", AF&S 39(1)) and a Leiden dissertation comparing Netherlands, Sweden, Finland (finds none fits Huntington's strict separation). Authors not captured: treat as a gap.
- Eisenhower farewell address: the Eisenhower Library page links the text (reading copy and press release).
- Other facts in this file (text of Rev 13, 17, 18 in KJV; names of scholars; arguments) are from the model's background knowledge and are flagged "check" where uncertain.
